### 2. ★★★☆☆ More than 1,300 arrested as clashes follow another day of mass protests across France

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T10:38:15+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Hundreds of thousands took part in demonstrations in France, with protests also taking place in other European countries.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch: BBC in Paris as police charge protesters during skirmishes
More than 1,300 arrests were made as hundreds of thousands of people took part in another day of protests across France on Saturday that were followed by some clashes later in the day, officials have said.
After largely peaceful demonstrations, police fired tear gas and water cannon to disperse protesters in a number of cities including Paris, while barricades were set alight in Marseille and people ripped boards from a shop in Bordeaux.
Officials have so far managed to avoid rioting and looting on the scale seen last weekend, which resulted in some of the worst scenes of disorder in France&#x27;s modern history.
The interior ministry said an estimated 410,000 people protested on Saturday, while the unions put the number at about 800,000.
Katya Adler: France divided over who to blame for riots and what to learn from them
Some 70,000 police officers were deployed and restrictions imposed in several cities to prevent the latest protests over the cost of living and poor school conditions from descending into the kind of chaos which saw officers outnumbered, hundreds of businesses looted and buildings torched.
The interior ministry said 53 officers were hurt in clashes across the country on Saturday.
French President Emmanuel Macron had decreed earlier this week that only demonstrations organised by a recognised trade unions would be permitted. Hundreds of people have been jailed for vandalism, arson and looting after fast-track court hearings.
Image caption, In the southern port of Marseille, protesters erected makeshift street barricades and set them alight late on Saturday
Clashes between protesters and riot police in Paris erupted at the end of a peaceful march from the city&#x27;s Porte d&#x27;Italie towards Porte de Vincennes, where the police operation included an elite unit that usually deals with organised crime and counter-terrorism.
Some protesters were seen erecting makeshift barricades and pelting officers with projectiles, while police baton-charged groups of masked youths who were smashing the windows of a car dealership.
French Interior Minister Laurent NuÃ±ez said that "several thousand radical activists gathered at the rear of the march, including around 500 individuals identified as rioters", news agency AFP reported.
The Interior Ministry said 142 people were arrested in the French capital on Saturday, less than half of the number arrested on the same day last week.
Image caption, Protesters clash with police at a demonstration in Bordeaux
In the southern port of Marseille, rubbish bins were set ablaze and metal sheets used to block streets. A law enforcement vehicle was seen driving through a burning barricade in France&#x27;s second-largest city.
Meanwhile, some protesters in dark clothing and face coverings were seen taking boards off a building to build a street barricade in the southern city of Bordeaux.
In Lyon, police used tear gas grenades to disperse a crowd which had reportedly gathered in an area deemed off-limits by the local prefecture.
"There were around 150 of us, mostly secondary school and university students. Then the tear gas came almost immediately. You could barely see anything in the street," said one protester in France&#x27;s third-largest city.
NuÃ±ez also said two people had been "seriously injured": one in the south-western city of Toulouse and another one in Clermont-Ferrand, central France. He said the number of arrests across the country would likely rise.
The man in Toulouse, a 20-year-old protester, had several fingers torn off one hand when a nearby explosive went off, Franceinfo reported, citing the medic who treated him.
As the unrest continued into the early hours of Sunday morning, authorities in Bouches-du-RhÃ´ne, the region in which Marseilles sits, said seven businesses had been "looted or damaged".
Image caption, "We really want a change in France," a teenage protester told the BBC
Earlier, a peaceful protester in Paris who gave her name as Zelie told the BBC people had taken to the streets because they were "really frustrated" and had been "suffering a lot" since Macron came to office.
"They keep us quiet. They take our money, they tax us a lot and we can&#x27;t say anything. Today we are in the streets because it&#x27;s been too long, it&#x27;s been too much," the 26-year-old said.
Two masked teenage protesters, who did not want to give their names, also said their generation was in despair and felt abandoned by the government.
"I think looting is really not good," one of the demonstrators said of last Saturday&#x27;s disorder. "But it&#x27;s because of poverty and the violence of the police."
He added that the protests would end "when we have a response to our demands. We really want a change in France".
Meanwhile, some 13,000 people joined a rally against the hard-right National Rally (RN) party in the north-central city of OrlÃ©ans, which proceeded peacefully.
The recent unrest has played out against a backdrop of a polarised political landscape, with France preparing for what could be the most divisive election period in a generation.
Macron will leave office next spring and polls suggest French voters will ultimately face a choice between RN&#x27;s candidate Marine Le Pen and that of the radical-left La France Insoumise (France Unbowed).
Leading figures from both parties have levelled the blame for the disorder at Macron, though right-wing figures have also heaped criticism on rioters while left-wing leaders support the union-led protests.
LFI candidate Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon said French media had not covered the peaceful protests while police had created "a setup deliberately designed to provoke chaos" that had "mutilated two people".
Mass protests triggered by similar issues as in France also rocked several other European cities on Saturday.
In Barcelona, more than 120 people were arrested after a student protest descended into what local authorities described as "gratuitous violence".
In Albania&#x27;s capital Tirana, riot police fired tear gas to disperse thousands of anti-government demonstrators, while clashes were also reported in Belgium&#x27;s capital Brussels.
In Milan, northern Italy, a major security operation including hundreds of soldiers alongside police prevented protesters gathering in the city. Many had feared a repeat of violent scenes last week when some started fires, smashed windows and looted shops.
Image caption, People clash with police during a demonstration against the high cost of living in Bordeaux.
Around 550,000 people took part in last weekend&#x27;s demonstrations against rising fuel prices and the cost of living, with frustrations escalating after weeks of student protests. The chaos which followed resulted in more than 2,500 arrests.
Marches were initially largely peaceful but became marred by scenes of violence, looting and destruction, with more than 2,000 shops and other businesses across France said to have been directly impacted. The damage was estimated "at hundreds of millions euros", France&#x27;s Chambers of Commerce and Industry (CCI) said.
One demonstrator&#x27;s hand was blown off by a police crowd-control grenade, according to AFP, while about 1,000 police officers were injured.
The French authorities later said they were forced to impose overnight curfews in Paris and other cities.
The continuing protests come ahead of France&#x27;s presidential elections in six months&#x27; time, and leading contenders have levelled the blame at Macron and his ministers.

### 4. ★★★☆☆ Obama’s Ohio, Michigan speeches: Key takeaways on Trump, Iran war, midterms - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / GN Top / Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T08:51:07+00:00
- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMisgFBVV95cUxQNmpMTzVMV0M5WU04b2s2LW1QeWlLWVVSdnRJQ2xQeW9WeVdXVjZYVkVwVFgzWTd5U25HZHJ4VFFIb0xfZDBrOVNJYzVsWndCcmhfdEkycExMaVoxelNzcWtuTWhxdjFKWVZxeVhGemVZdi0wNjBaX1pwdWE0aTBOS1gtUlNUcjRwRXNzcXNkdnF6ZDlrTFBJejVCQ1kwUGRjQWQ2MTNHai10OUlhQ29TdFNn0gG3AUFVX3lxTE9zVTVQVDdCLWlial92eERzejVzYnlMSnRwdTRiOFd5WTZCcFE2VnVabkJYOFNwV0NQRl90a0R1ZllSc2NDUDdPLVd3bXBUV0dLcU01a0MzbVhNVUlHczgzUTlYOW95YkZFVVdFOW8zVmROUUN0alc1aTZzRnNuTUxZcHg1a1VTQktVYkNhSTBNMzZiampRSUFtbj

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share ‘Unnecessary’ Iran war, corruption: Takeaways from Obama’s midterm speeches on social media
Obama joins El-Sayed in Michigan rally cry before key midterms
Former President Barack Obama has railed against the Trump administration on a range of issues, including dragging the United States into “an unnecessary war” against Iran, as he campaigned for Democrats in Ohio and Michigan ahead of the November 3 midterm elections.
Speaking at rallies on Saturday, Obama accused President Donald Trump of “chaos and corruption”, as he urged voters to reject Republicans.
list of 2 items list 1 of 2 Obama joins El-Sayed in Michigan rally cry before key midterms
list 2 of 2 Obama slams Trump’s ‘cruelty and corruption’ as he rallies Democrats
The speeches came just 10 days before the elections, with Senate and gubernatorial races taking place in both states.
Obama blames Trump’s Iran war for rising fuel prices
“The current president chose to make a series of bad decisions,” Obama said in Cleveland, Ohio, where he rallied in support of former Democratic Senator Sherrod Brown.
“Most importantly, he decided to start an unnecessary war that nobody asked for,” Obama said.
The former president defended the 2015 nuclear agreement reached under his administration, which restricted Tehran’s nuclear programme in exchange for sanctions relief.
“Iran didn’t have nuclear weapons because I had put a deal in place that everybody acknowledged kept them from getting nukes, and I didn’t have to fire a missile to do it. I just had to engage in diplomacy,” Obama said.
Obama blamed the conflict for rising oil and diesel prices, citing attacks on US military bases, American casualties and disruptions to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz.
“Our allies and our bases have been attacked. Americans have been killed, hundreds of Americans have been wounded. We have run down our stock of missiles, and Iran now has veto power over the Strait of Hormuz where oil goes through to the world market,” he said.
The disruptions in oil flow through the strait, located between Iran and Oman, have pushed up energy prices worldwide. Before the war, a fifth of global oil and gas passed through the strait, which Iran has used as leverage in the war.
“This is the reason why the price of oil is up … This is the reason the price of diesel is up.”
The spike in diesel and petrol prices will likely cost the Republican Party, as the majority of Americans hold Trump responsible for starting the war and the subsequent increase in prices.
Obama also mocked Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth over his focus on military culture and the Pentagon’s plans to livestream the execution of Nidal Hasan, convicted of killing 13 people at Fort Hood in 2009.
Tariffs are making life more expensive for Americans
Obama criticised Trump’s tariffs, saying Americans were paying more for goods, including products made in the US using imported parts.
“When you impose tariffs, consumers pay the tab,” he said.
“It’s not just the goods that you use directly that have been imported … It’s also the car you build here in America. They might need a part from somewhere else, and now suddenly there’s a tariff on it.”
He questioned Trump’s trade dispute with Canada, a major US trading partner.
The two countries have been locked in a trade dispute, with Trump threatening 50 percent tariffs on Canadian vehicles and auto parts from January 2027.
“And the big trade war he’s starting now is with that well-known evil empire Canada,” Obama said sarcastically.
“Canadians are the nicest people … they’re like our friendly neighbours to the north, and he’s like picking a fight with them.”
Obama also pointed to the growing US national debt, which has reached $40 trillion.
“Instead of bringing down deficits like he said with these tariffs, we’re now running a debt to the tune of $40 trillion.”
The figure refers to the total federal debt, which has accumulated over successive administrations, rather than the annual budget deficit.
Obama accused the Trump administration of favouring wealthy Americans while common households struggled with rising costs.
“After two years of chaos and cruelty and corruption and incompetence, the likes of which we have not seen in the modern era, you get a chance to say, ‘Enough is enough,&#x27;” Obama declared. “We are voting for change right here in Ohio.”
He pointed to Trump’s 2025 tax and spending legislation, which extended tax cuts while reducing federal spending on programmes including Medicaid and food assistance.
“The only bill they passed of any significance in the last two years was a trillion-dollar tax break that mostly benefitted the richest Americans,” he said.
Obama also took aim at Trump’s White House renovations, including plans for a ballroom, changes to the Rose Garden and the addition of gold decorations.
“He seems very focused on home renovations. He should be on HGTV,” Obama joked.
“He’s got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory, just gold everywhere.”
The former president said the US needed secure borders but criticised the methods used by immigration agents under the Trump administration.
“I believe that we do need to make sure that our border is orderly and secure and that people who want to come here do so lawfully,” he said.
“But I don’t recall anybody campaigning on sending masked agents running through our streets. I don’t recall anybody talking about law-abiding citizens being arrested, detained, or shot.”
“That’s not what we have to do to get our borders under control,” he added.
Obama attacks Ramaswamy over controversial Amy Acton advertisement
In Ohio, Obama criticised Republican gubernatorial candidate Vivek Ramaswamy over a campaign advertisement featuring the estranged mother of his Democratic opponent, Amy Acton.
The advertisement features Acton’s mother questioning her honesty and her account of her childhood, without mentioning the allegations of sexual abuse that led to their separation decades ago.
It has drawn criticism from Republicans, including Ohio Governor Mike DeWine, although Trump and Vice President JD Vance have defended it.
Obama condemned Ramaswamy’s decision to use Acton’s family history in the campaign.
“There’s got to be something inside you where you just say, you know, no office is worth stooping that low,” he said.
He also praised Acton’s career in public service and urged Ohio voters to support her on November 3.
Obama urges Michigan voters to back El-Sayed and Benson
In Detroit, Obama campaigned for Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed and gubernatorial contender Jocelyn Benson.
He praised El-Sayed’s Michigan roots and criticised his Republican opponent, Mike Rogers, over his support for the US-Israel war on Iran and Trump’s trade policies.
“Abdul is as Michigan as it gets,” Obama said, referring to his upbringing in the state, his family’s ties to the auto industry and his involvement in school sports.
Obama also spoke about attacks on El-Sayed’s Muslim identity, comparing them with false claims about his own religion and birthplace during his presidential campaigns.
“Unlike Mike Rogers, Abdul hasn’t spent months cheerleading the Iran war … or called these tariffs ‘masterful’.”
The former president pushed back against those who call El-Sayed a “radical”, declaring that “Abdul shares my values”.
Obama also praised Benson for standing by Michigan’s 2020 election results despite threats and protests outside her home.
“When armed protesters showed up outside her home in 2020, demanded she overturn Michigan’s fair and free election results, Jocelyn stood her ground, stood up for you and your rights,” he said.
Obama calls midterms a ‘circuit breaker’ against Trump
Obama said the midterm elections offered voters a chance to limit Trump’s power during the remaining two years of his presidency.
“This election is our last best chance to at least rein in some of the foolishness of this administration,” he said. “It’s the circuit breaker in the system. We need to flip that circuit breaker.”
He also warned that dishonesty, intimidation and bullying were becoming accepted in US politics.
“We are not going to fix the economic system or the political system … so long as we’ve got a culture where anything goes and where people in the highest offices in the land think it’s OK to lie and cheat and bully,” he said.
“What we are missing in our public discourse is just basic decency.”

### 5. ★★★☆☆ America's Miscalculation: Why even Increased Oil Flows through Hormuz won't Solve the Energy Challenge - Informed Comment

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T04:15:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: America's Miscalculation: Why even Increased Oil Flows through Hormuz won't Solve the Energy Challenge&nbsp;&nbsp;Informed Comment

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Tehran (Special to Informed Comment; Feature) &#8211; Donald Trump has pointed to rising oil flows through the Strait of Hormuz as evidence that the energy crisis is easing. The figures offer some support for this view. In September, crude exports from the Middle East recovered to near pre-war levels on some days. Yet oil prices have remained high. On October 16, Brent crude reached $109.86 a barrel, its highest level since May. This gap between recovering exports and rising prices reveals a basic problem in Washington's approach: the volume of oil moving through the strait is only one part of the energy market.
Eastern crude exports reached an average of 14-16 million barrels per day on September. This rate suggest that exporters have found ways to move more oil despite the risks. But they do not mean that the market has returned to its previous condition.
Some of the recovery has depended on changes in shipping routes, the use of more tankers, and transfers of oil between ships outside the strait. These arrangements help maintain exports, but they also add complexity and cost. As Reuters reported on October 5 , the increase in flows has taken place alongside rising maritime risks and logistical limits.
The difference matters. A barrel of oil leaving the Persian Gulf does not reach its destination at the same cost, speed, or level of reliability as before the war. The market responds to the cost and security of delivery, not simply to the number of barrels passing through the Strait.
The cost of shipping and insurance is one of the clearest signs of this problem. On October 12, oil-producing countries in the OPEC+ group warned that shipping and insurance costs had risen to more than one-third of the value of the commodities being transported. Such costs make energy trade more expensive even when physical flows recover.
Tanker rates also remain unusually high. According to Reuters , rates for super tankers fell from their recent record levels but remained close to those highs. Insurance companies and shipping firms must consider what might happen during a voyage. A vessel may pass safely today, but the possibility of an attack tomorrow still affects insurance decisions, operating costs, and the willingness of companies to send ships into the region. These costs remain high even when more oil is moving. They also feed into the price paid by buyers, particularly when supplies are already tight and companies have few easy alternatives.
The deeper problem is uncertainty. Oil prices reflect current supply and demand, but they are also shaped by expectations about future supply and the ability of traders and consumers to manage risk.
A recent Federal Reserve analysis explains how a major supply shock changes the relationship between immediate oil prices and futures prices. When supplies become difficult to secure, buyers place greater value on having oil available now. Market liquidity can also weaken, making it harder to trade without affecting prices.
This helps explain why a rise in oil flows does not bring prices down quickly. Buyers need confidence that supplies will remain available in the coming weeks and months. If they expect another disruption such as war, they may continue to compete for secure supplies and pay more to protect themselves against future shortages.
Recent events reinforce these concerns. new attacks on ships near Hormuz and threats to Saudi energy infrastructure and more importantly news about coming war, have added to fears that supply could be disrupted again. The price increase cannot be explained by market psychology alone. Physical risks remain real. But those risks affect prices partly because they change what companies expect to happen next.
Trump's focus on oil flows treats the crisis mainly as a problem of quantity: if more oil passes through Hormuz, supply should improve and prices should fall. That logic misses the cost of restoring confidence in the energy system.
File photo, Kuwaiti oil tanker. US military: PHP Thomas Tolliver. Public Domain. Via Picryl
The distinction also matters for US policy. Military action may protect some shipping routes or reduce an immediate threat, but it cannot guarantee that insurers will lower premiums, or market will stop worrying about another escalation. A statement that exports " have recovered " cannot replace evidence that trade is becoming safer and more predictable. The lesson is not that oil flows do not matter. They matter greatly. The problem is assuming that increased flows are enough to solve the challenge.
Until confidence returns, higher exports will not bring lower oil prices &mdash;and, in turn, lower prices for diesel and other refined fuels. Trump can point to rising volumes of oil moving through the Strait, but the numbers alone cannot prevent prices from remaining high or even rising further. Oil prices are shaped not simply by how much oil reaches the market, but by what markets expect to happen next . And this is a challenge for which the United States has no solution, at least for now.
Mohammad Amin Ahmadi is a PhD candidate in International Relations specializing in Iran’s foreign policy, regional security, and Foreign Policy Analysis. His research examines foreign policy decision-making and the evolving political and security order in the Middle East. He has published academic research on Iranian foreign policy and regional affairs.

### 8. ★★★☆☆ Why is Ukraine so reliant on Starlink and can Europe plug the gap?

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T05:45:31+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukraine needs another satellite internet service, but a decent alternative to Elon Musk's network doesn't exist - yet.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, A man offers residents in Kherson, Ukraine, the use of his Starlink internet after a Russian strike
Ukraine is in desperate need of a new satellite internet service, but a fast and reliable alternative to Elon Musk&#x27;s Starlink network simply doesn&#x27;t exist - yet.
Last week&#x27;s sudden slowdown of the service in Ukraine, which affected Kyiv&#x27;s ability to defend itself from Russian attacks, has shown how vulnerable the country can be, and sharply increased Europe&#x27;s urgency to have its own military-grade satellite network.
That vulnerability was highlighted by Musk himself on Saturday, when he accused Ukraine of violating Starlink&#x27;s terms of service by using it for "drone strikes to kill people".
Saying that loophole was closed last week, he wrote on X , external : "Violation of those terms will result in end of service."
He added: "Were Starlink to be turned off, the Ukrainian front line would collapse immediately".
There are a few alternatives, but they are all lagging behind Starlink, which is the world&#x27;s largest satellite constellation.
France&#x27;s Eutelsat, with its OneWeb internet service, is the main alternative - but it only has about 600 active satellites, compared with Starlink&#x27;s 10,000-plus.
The EU is working on a new network to rival Starlink, IrisÂ², however it won&#x27;t be up and running until 2029.
For Ukraine&#x27;s troops on the ground, several of whom spoke anonymously to the BBC, reliable connectivity is the difference between life and death.
"God forbid they shut it off completely, it would be an absolute catastrophe," said one. "I don&#x27;t give a damn how much an alternative costs when our lives are on the line."
For now, Ukraine&#x27;s best bet is to join Govsatcom, the EU&#x27;s satellite sharing network - but it cannot be used for military warfare like controlling drones.
Figure caption, Watch: Who controls Ukraine&#x27;s access to Starlink?
Starlink and its military-grade service, Starshield, are both owned by Musk&#x27;s SpaceX company and have dominated the global satellite market for years.
Ukraine relies on Starlink for communications on the battlefield and Starshield, which is controlled by the US government, for military purposes like drone warfare. The network is able to provide fast, real-time connectivity, which is crucial for defence and intercepting Russian attacks.
"You can&#x27;t employ drone warfare without this kind of high-speed internet connection," said Vincent Tourret, a researcher at the French Institute of International Relations.
Last week&#x27;s slowdown, which has only just been restored , highlighted the risks of relying on a US provider at a time when tensions between Kyiv and Washington continue to deteriorate.
"Ukraine&#x27;s defence cannot depend on Trump&#x27;s or Musk&#x27;s whims," French MEP, Christophe Grudler, tweeted.
Who authorised the Starlink restrictions is still not clear, with a confusing blame-game ongoing between the Pentagon, Donald Trump and Musk.
While Starlink has been one of Ukraine&#x27;s greatest assets in this conflict, the "flipside of that dependence" has been exposed, explained Kari Bingen, director of the Aerospace Security Project at CSIS.
"This is a critical military capability that Ukraine relies on, but doesn&#x27;t control."
Image source, Dmytro Smolienko/ Ukrinform/ Getty Images
Image caption, Ukrainian soldiers set up a Starlink terminal on the battlefield
Starlink is not the only satellite network Ukraine can access, so if it goes down there are other options - but experts say they aren&#x27;t as fast, and troops the BBC spoke to agree.
Starlink "gives us the upper hand when it comes to passing commands - even one or two seconds are critical when we can transfer data and orders faster, and you can really feel that advantage on the battlefield", a soldier said.
The terminals used to connect to the satellites are also an important factor. Starlink&#x27;s are relatively small and lightweight, so they can be fixed to any kind of vehicle or drone.
Eutelsat&#x27;s terminals "are slightly larger, so they can be fitted on the roof of a car, or on tanks, but they can&#x27;t necessarily sit on small drones", said Eva Berneke, former CEO of the French satellite company.
They are also about five times more expensive, she added.
Rhetoric from the White House - like Trump&#x27;s scathing criticism of Nato and his threats against Greenland - have given the EU "some pretty brutal alarm bells", Berneke told the BBC.
She had only been at Eutelsat for a month when Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022, and said she noticed a marked change in urgency from European authorities, as they looked to her company to help plug holes left by Starlink if it could not be relied upon.
Comments from Musk have further heightened the need for an alternative: "Be quiet, small man... there is no substitute for Starlink," he tweeted , external at Poland&#x27;s foreign minister last year, when the politician said he would "look for other suppliers" if SpaceX was unreliable.
Europe has realised its security depends on space, and it must "strengthen its satellite capacities, urgently", a spokesperson for the European Commission told the BBC.
Right now, it has Govsatcom, a network that was set up in January to share existing satellites between EU member states. Ukraine is not a full-fledged member (although it is trying to become one), but the Commission is in talks with Kyiv to join Govsatcom.
But even if Ukraine could connect tomorrow, the network could not be used for warfare operations like piloting drones, which Starshield is used for.
That could change by the start of next year, however, when the European Commission hopes commercial companies, like Eutelsat&#x27;s OneWeb, will join the programme. This will give Ukraine some - but not all - of its military network needs.
Image caption, French President Emmanuel Macron, with a Eutelsat terminal, at the International Paris Air Show last year
Eva Berneke told the BBC she is confident the company will be able to "support quite a lot of Ukraine&#x27;s key military needs". But meeting its communications needs is another thing because, as is the case with most countries, they are varied and massive.
Eutelsat could not comment on Govsatcom, but told the BBC that "as the only European operator of a fully operational [Low Earth Orbit] constellation, we are committed to providing resilient connectivity to the businesses, governments and communities we serve".
No timeline has been given for when Ukraine will be able to join the Govsatcom network. When it does it will likely be as a reliable back-up to Starlink and Starshield.
But that&#x27;s better than nothing, Vincent Tourret said. "If Starlink is shut down, it&#x27;s good to have another way to stay connected, but it won&#x27;t be enough for strikes or interceptions, at this stage."
Govsatcom is seen as a short-term safety net, until the stronger and more secure network, IrisÂ² , external is up and running in 2029.
The ambitious project (pronounced Iris squared ) is Europe&#x27;s answer to Starlink and Starshield. It will be a defence-first programme, the European Commission says, with a focus on governments, military and emergency services. Ukraine has been guaranteed access to it from day one.
Part of the EU&#x27;s space programme, the European satellite companies Eutelsat, SES and Hispasat are leading the project in a consortium called SpaceRISE.
It will still have far fewer satellites than Starlink, with 348 across low and medium earth orbit - but that shouldn&#x27;t be seen as a negative, said Kari Bingen.
"Think about a flash light beaming down - those satellites will be able to cover a greater amount of the Earth, so you need fewer of them," she explained. "They&#x27;re all trade offs - I would not take it as a negative, that IrisÂ² is planning for less satellites."
Tourret was more sceptical: "It&#x27;s no gamechanger and no great saviour, I&#x27;m afraid".
Image caption, A SpaceX Falcon 9 rocket, carrying 56 Starlink satellites, takes off from Cape Canaveral in March
A highly produced video on the EU Space Policy&#x27;s website declares: "With IrisÂ², Europe will no longer depend on others for satellite connectivity."
So does that mean Ukraine could switch off Starlink? No - that wouldn&#x27;t be a good idea.
"Users need to think about communications resilience," Bingen said. "So if something happens to one system, you have other ways to maintain connectivity."
It&#x27;s similar to how mobile phones work, explained Eva Berneke: "If you don&#x27;t have 5G, you go back to 4G. And if you don&#x27;t have 4G, you might use 3G, so you have multiple networks for resilience. It&#x27;s the same idea in warfare."
But while a phone might not load pictures as quickly on 3G, for a drone operator, it can be deadly: "That is not a nice situation to have to be in when you&#x27;re fighting a war."
IrisÂ² is not the only option. This week, Finnish companies Nokia and ICEYE , external announced they would look at developing satellite communications for Ukraine&#x27;s military defences.
And a joint venture between European satellite makers Airbus, Leonardo and Thales, called Project Bromo, is hoped to be another competitor against SpaceX, but it is still in the very early phases.
Space projects take a long time, Berneke said, and need " a lot of money".
Designing the programme, making the satellites, launching them and then having enough of them in orbit for full coverage takes many years.
"Even if you gave me â¬8bn right now, I don&#x27;t think I could have anything up there before 2032-2033," she explained.
Military work in Space is nothing new, and has been happening since the dawn of the space age in the 1950s. But the conflict in Ukraine has shown how integral it is to modern warfare.
"Communication is so important, and terrestrial communication platforms, whether it&#x27;s 5G or fibre, can be attacked on the ground," Berneke said. "It is a lot harder to shoot down an entire satellite network. That&#x27;s not something any military can do right now."
While we look to the skies, however, most of the world&#x27;s communications are travelling via undersea cables - about 99% in fact. But these are vulnerable to attack - this week the UK government outlined new measures to protect its cables, after an increase in suspicious activity by Russian vessels.
"In a crisis or conflict, there are serious concerns that undersea cables will be cut... so if that happens it&#x27;s good to have satellite communication as a backup," Kari Bingen said.

Felicity Bradstock is a writer and journalist based in Mexico City. She writes for energy websites and covers several other industries, as well as writing&#8230;
Syria's Energy Comeback Could Reshape Middle East Oil and Gas
Syria is attracting international investment to revive its oil and gas production and rebuild its electricity infrastructure.
Major solar, wind, and hydropower projects could significantly expand the country's electricity generation capacity.
Disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz are increasing interest in Syria as an alternative energy transportation corridor.
Syria is undergoing countrywide reconstruction after more than a decade of conflict, which caused extensive infrastructure damage and led to millions of people being displaced. As Syrians rebuild their lives, the government has big plans for economic diversification and growth to support long-term recovery. Thanks to its favourable geographic position, this includes the development of a strong energy sector that could help eventually establish Syria as a regional hub.
Syria is now led by President Ahmed al-Sharaa, who formed a transitional government on March 29, 2025, following the end of the Assad regime. The government has introduced a wide range of policies and reforms over the past year, across all sectors, aimed at improving living standards and supporting economic growth. The national strategy focuses on institutional reorganisation, security integration, economic stabilisation, and transitional justice ahead of planned national elections in 2030.
The transitional government has taken control over Syrian energy, including the Omar oil field and Tabiyeh gas complex. This follows over a decade during which Syria’s energy sector was split, with Damascus situated far from the country’s oil-rich northeast. For years, while the government still oversaw gas output in the country’s central region, it lost control of some of the country’s largest production sites.
A 2026 agreement between the transitional government and the Kurdish-led Syrian Democratic Forces (SDF) provides the potential to integrate SDF forces into state structures, which could further unify energy governance. Meanwhile, the lifting of United States and European Union sanctions on Syria, following the fall of the Assad regime, and the relaunch of the SWIFT transfer system have made it easier for international companies to invest in Syria.
Before the conflict, which commenced in 2011, Syria was producing an average of 380,000 bpd of crude and 900 million cubic feet of gas. However, the conflict dramatically reduced output, with oil production down 80 per cent and gas by half, according to figures from Wood Mackenzie. Nevertheless, Syria is still home to estimated reserves of 1.3 billion barrels of oil equivalent, in addition to having vast unexplored onshore areas and an entirely untapped offshore sector. Higher levels of foreign investment could support an accelerated recovery in the oil and gas sector.
In May, Syria announced that it had an offshore site for its first deep-water oil and gas exploration project to be developed by the U.S. major Chevron, Qatar’s UCC Holding, and the Syrian Petroleum Company (SPC). This supports the government’s aim to attract greater foreign investment in Syria’s energy sector. Other U.S. oil and gas firms have also expressed interest in Syrian oil and gas projects under the transitional government, opening the door to new investment opportunities.
There is significant potential for Syria’s energy industry given the current geopolitical challenges to global energy. Iran’s restrictions on energy trade through the Strait of Hormuz have spurred interest in Syria as a potential transit route for pipelines transporting Persian Gulf oil and gas to the Mediterranean. Syria’s border with Turkey offers enhanced access to major pipeline networks, which could facilitate the transport of oil and gas from the Middle East to Europe. With greater investment, Syria could serve as an “energy land bridge” for Hormuz-bypass pipelines, according to the Baker Institute .
In addition to redeveloping its oil and gas assets and potentially acting as a regional energy trade hub, the transitional government also has major plans for Syria’s renewable energy sector. Small-scale solar projects have already begun to emerge, with estimates suggesting that the country’s off-grid solar capacity increased from around 250 MW in 2022 to over 2 GW in 2025.
Syria is looking to develop both its solar and wind power sectors. In June 2025, the government signed a $7 billion deal with a consortium from Qatar, Turkey, and the United States to rebuild its power sector, including four gas-fired power plants and a 1 GW solar park. The project is expected to add 5 GW of capacity, which will cover almost half of the current electricity demand.
The government signed a Joint Development Agreement with the Saudi energy group ACWA Power in September 2025, as they work to identify suitable locations for wind energy projects, aiming to develop 1 GW of solar PV capacity and approximately 1.5 GW of wind power, as well as utility-scale battery storage to enhance reliability.
In February, the Syrian Ministry of Energy also signed an agreement with the Dubai-based energy group Marv Energy to develop a 700-MW wind farm with plans to install 140 locally manufactured wind turbines across several sites to connect to the national electricity grid. Marv Energy plans to manufacture the turbines at its factory in the city of Homs in western Syria. It aims to complete the project within five to seven years.
In October, the Ministry of Energy issued a request for expressions of interest for the Halabiya-Zalabiya Dam project on the Euphrates River. The project would include the construction of an 81-MW hydropower plant and a 1.2-GW pumped-storage hydropower facility, with an estimated investment cost of $1.16 billion. The project would require the development of a 1,200-metre-long, 23-metre-high dam. Meanwhile, the hydropower plant would have three 27-MW turbines.
Syria has significant oil, gas, and renewable energy capacity, but it is highly dependent on foreign investment to support development. Due to ongoing limitations to energy trade via the Strait of Hormuz, several investors are looking to Syria to develop alternative trade routes between the Middle East and Europe, which could support the country’s economic recovery.

### 13. ★★★☆☆ President Donald Trump Just Proclaimed, "Tariffs Don't Cause Inflation, They Cause Wealth," but Data From Federal Reserve Economists Show Otherwise - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T08:26:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: President Donald Trump Just Proclaimed, "Tariffs Don't Cause Inflation, They Cause Wealth," but Data From Federal Reserve Economists Show Otherwise&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance</fon

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

President Donald Trump Just Proclaimed, "Tariffs Don't Cause Inflation, They Cause Wealth," but Data From Federal Reserve Economists Show Otherwise
From a purely statistical standpoint, Wall Street has enjoyed having President Donald Trump in the White House. Even accounting for a few periods of historic volatility (e.g., the COVID-19 crash in February-March 2020 and the tariff tantrum of April 2025), the average annual return of the Dow Jones Industrial Average (DJINDICES:^DJI), S&P 500 (SNPINDEX:^GSPC), and Nasdaq Composite (NASDAQINDEX:^IXIC) has been higher under Trump than under most presidents since the late 1890s.
However, this doesn't mean that all of the president's policies are necessarily well-received on Wall Street. For example, Trumpflation (inflation specifically driven by Donald Trump's policies) is a genuine threat to the artificial intelligence (AI)-driven bull market.
Missed AI's "Act 1"? Act 2 Could Be 14x Bigger. Most investors think they missed the AI boat because they didn't buy Nvidia in 2005. But according to our analysts, we're only at the end of "Act 1"—the R&D phase. "Act 2" is the global rollout. Continue »
President Trump's tariffs are doing more harm than good. Image source: Official White House Photo by Daniel Torok.
President Trump sees things differently. While speaking to an audience at a campaign rally in Montana ahead of the Nov. 3 midterm elections, Trump touched on inflation and the role his tariff and trade policy may have played in it. Said the president:
We don't have inflation, and tariffs don't cause inflation. You know what they cause? Wealth.
Although Trump insists that tariffs aren't contributing to persistently elevated inflation and are beneficial to the U.S. economy, reports from New York Federal Reserve economists show otherwise .
Tariffs have been foundational to Donald Trump's presidency
While tariffs have been a hot-button topic since Trump's second non-consecutive term began in January 2025, they've been a theme throughout both of his terms. In 2018, the Trump administration kicked off a trade war with China, imposing tariffs on washing machines, solar panels, steel, aluminum, and various tech goods.
To encourage overseas companies to manufacture their U.S.-bound products in America
To make U.S. products more price-competitive with those being imported
Shortly after the start of his second term, he introduced a new round of tariffs. Trump's April 2025 "Liberation Day" tariffs imposed a sweeping global duty and higher reciprocal tariffs on dozens of countries deemed to have adverse trade imbalances with America. A majority of these tariffs, imposed under the International Emergency Economic Powers Act, were invalidated by the U.S. Supreme Court in February 2026.
In late July, the Trump administration imposed new sweeping global duties, ranging from 10% to 12.5%, on more than 80 countries under Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974. These new tariffs cover more than 99% of U.S. imports.
While President Trump has frequently praised tariffs for bringing in extra revenue, New York Federal Reserve economists have found that the president's tariffs are doing more harm than good.
Donald Trump's tariffs are raising consumer prices, according to Federal Reserve economists
Recently, three NY Federal Reserve economists writing for Liberty Street Economics dug into the impact of President Trump's 2025 tariffs on consumer prices. In "How Fast Do Tariffs Pass Through into Consumer Prices," the trio of authors found clear evidence that tariffs cause inflation.
According to the report, every percentage point increase in average tariffs equated to a quarter-percentage-point increase in consumer goods prices after one year. Economists estimate that Trump's tariffs have added 2.9 percentage points to goods price inflation by February 2026. Here's the kicker: economists estimate that goods prices would have modestly declined without the president's tariffs.
The authors homed in on three catalysts that have lifted consumer prices:
Tariffs directly raise prices for imported goods, with overseas companies rarely absorbing tariff costs.
Higher input costs. Adding duties to unfinished imported goods (e.g., steel) used to complete the manufacture of a product domestically can increase production costs, which are then passed on to consumers.
In some instances where tariffs made imported goods notably pricier than U.S.-made products, domestic manufacturers would increase their prices to capture some of this difference.
A separate Liberty Street Economics report from December 2024 ("Do Import Tariffs Protect U.S. Firms?") also uncovered negative correlations from Donald Trump's 2018 China tariffs.
The four economists who contributed to this report highlighted the effects of input tariffs in raising consumer prices. Perhaps more importantly, the authors found a clear correlation between public companies whose stock declined on China tariff announcement days and worse future real outcomes.
The public companies that performed poorly on tariff announcement days were associated with declines in employment, labor productivity, sales, and profits from 2019-2021. In other words, the adverse effects of Trump's tariffs endured for years after they were enacted.
President Trump's tariffs are one of the two policies directly lifting consumer prices at the moment. The Trump-led Iran war is the other catalyst. The ongoing closure of the Strait of Hormuz has led to the largest energy supply disruption in modern times, sending gas and diesel prices soaring.
Federal Reserve policymakers have been left with little choice but to undertake only the fourth interest rate-hiking cycle of the 21st century. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and 11 other members of the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) voted unanimously to raise the federal funds target rate by 25 basis points to 3.75%-4.00% on Sept. 16.
However, the FOMC's battle against persistently elevated inflation is far from over. Sticky core inflation, which excludes volatile food and energy costs, coupled with near-record-high diesel prices, will likely prompt several additional rate hikes over the next year.
Historically, Fed rate-hiking cycles have coincided with strong economic growth and healthy bull markets. But this time may very well be different.
The stock market entered 2026 at its second-priciest valuation in history and can ill afford any speed bumps or potholes. If the FOMC gets aggressive with rate hikes to deliver price stability, the AI-driven stock market could pay the price. Even a relatively small slowdown in the AI data center build-out or AI growth projections due to higher interest rates may be enough to send the Dow Jones Industrial Average, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite over the edge.

Consider when Netflix made this list on December 17, 2004... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $355,124 !* Or when Nvidia made this list on April 15, 2005... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $1,296,254 !*

Share Bulgarians vote for president in shadow of Russia’s war on Ukraine on social media
An election billboard of President Iliana Iotova and vice presidential candidate Kiril Valchev in Sofia, Bulgaria, on October 23, 2026 [Nikolay Doychinov/AFP]
Polls have opened in Bulgaria’s presidential election after a campaign that focused on growing economic hardship and drone attacks on Black Sea shipping.
Before Sunday’s vote, President Iliana Iotova, 61, and former caretaker Prime ⁠Minister Andrey Gyurov, 50, were leading a field of 27 candidates in the polls.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 War and heat: Why are wheat prices soaring?
list 2 of 3 Bulgaria searches for missing crew after drones hit two ships in Black Sea
list 3 of 3 Bulgaria ends search for missing crew after drone strike sinks cargo ship
⁠The presidency is a largely ceremonial post in Bulgaria. While major policies must be approved by parliament, direct election gives the president greater political authority. The president leads the armed forces and can veto legislation, sign international treaties and appoint ambassadors.
The election has also gained attention after a series of drone attacks on merchant ships off Bulgaria that likely killed about two dozen international seafarers.
The drone strikes ‌sparked international condemnation and concerns about the growing reach of Russia’s war on Ukraine, prompting Bulgaria to seek help from its allies to bolster its maritime security.
The Bulgarian government has stopped short of explicitly blaming Russia for the attacks, but Prime Minister Rumen Radev said the outcome of the strikes “benefits the Russian Federation”.
The two leading candidates for president have both condemned the strikes, but they differ on the broader war in Ukraine.
Gyurov, who served as caretaker prime minister this year, is backed by ⁠pro-Western, reformist parties and has long expressed support for Ukraine. He ⁠signed a security cooperation agreement with Kyiv during his time as premier.
“I want Bulgaria to have a voice of its own in Europe, a reliable NATO ally that defends its interests through preparedness, strong diplomacy and ⁠a clear position,” he said at a rally on Thursday.
A rescue operation is carried out at the ship Chelsea 3 near Byala, Bulgaria, on October 13, 2026, after it was hit by drones [Handout/Bulgarian Ministry of Interior via AP] Iotova, a former vice president, became acting president in January after Radev resigned to ⁠enter the parliamentary race, which he won on what many ⁠political analysts considered a Kremlin-friendly ticket.
Radev supports Iotova, and they both have called for a diplomatic end to the war.
“My opponents argue that there must be ‌a war of attrition,” Iotova said at a rally on Thursday. “The truth is, we are all the ones who are most exhausted.”
While there is a broad consensus among the parties in parliament on the country’s pro-Western path, widespread pro-Russian sentiments based on traditional historical and cultural ties with Russia have divided society, particularly since the start of Moscow’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.
Dobromir Zhivkov, managing director of the Market Links polling agency, said Iotova is among those who advocate that Bulgaria should take a more neutral position.
“They call for an end to aid for Ukraine and a halt to arms deliveries to Ukraine. We often hear them use Kremlin rhetoric and terminology borrowed directly from Kremlin propaganda,” he told The Associated Press news agency this past week.
Candidates are also addressing public fears about the steep rise in energy prices and surging inflation as many Bulgarians in rural areas struggle to make ends meet.
Polls by Alpha Research suggested Iotova would win 41 percent of the vote and ‌Gyurov ‌28 percent.
Voting started at 7am (05:00 GMT), and polls close at 8pm (18:00 GMT).
Analysts expected none of the 27 candidates to secure the outright majority needed to win in the first round, however.
Turnout must also be above 50 percent of the 6.6 million registered voters, and Sunday’s turnout is expected to be less than 40 percent, which means a likely run-off on November 1.

</details>

French and British Paratroopers jump from RAF A400 aircraft as part of military training exercise in Germany. Photograph: Andrew Grayson/BRITISH ARMY/AFP/Getty Images
French and British Paratroopers jump from RAF A400 aircraft as part of military training exercise in Germany. Photograph: Andrew Grayson/BRITISH ARMY/AFP/Getty Images
Ukraine war briefing: Kyiv targets Russian-occupied Crimea as UK and France conduct joint military exercise
Crimea strikes cause water and power cuts; war game designed to show how Nato countries could be defended amid fears of Russian aggression. What we know on day 1,705
A Ukrainian attack led to water and power cuts in occupied Crimea on Saturday, as a barrage of drones in several regions inside Russia left several dead, according to Russian authorities. Sergei Aksyonov, the Moscow-installed head of the Crimean peninsula, wrote on Telegram that due to the drone attack on local power grids “restrictions on the supply of electricity and water have been put in place”. He did not specify how many households had been affected in Crimea, which is home to more than 2.5 million people. The peninsula, which was seized from Ukraine by Russia in 2014, is a regular target for Ukrainian attacks.
A separate Ukrainian strike in part of the north-eastern Kharkiv region held by Russia killed at least three people and gravely wounded another on Saturday, the Moscow-backed authorities said.
Drones also rained down inside Russia . The country’s defence ministry said 616 of them had been shot down overnight across more than 20 regions of the country. During the day, drones hit Russia’s south-western Bryansk border region, killing at least three people, regional acting governor Yegor Kovalchuc said on Telegram.
In Ukraine, authorities said that at least five people died in several Russian drone and missile strikes in the country’s east. Dnipropetrovsk regional governor Oleksandr Ganzha on Saturday evening said three people were killed and at least 12 wounded when a Russian drone strike hit the town of Synelnykove, setting the fourth floor of a high-rise building on fire. Authorities in Sumy meanwhile said a Russian drone hit a farm, killing a 31-year-old woman and injuring four others.
Thousands of British and French troops have taken part in a military training exercise that included one of Britain’s largest airborne operations in two decades, the UK government said on Sunday. The war game is designed to show how Nato countries could be defended against a modern-day attack and comes during fears about Russian aggression in Europe amid Moscow’s war against Ukraine.
About 2,500 troops were involved in the seven-week exercise, in which 600 British and French paratroopers dropped from four RAF A400M Atlas transport aircraft over Germany. The exercise called Rhino Titan, held at the Sennelager military training centre in Germany, has been testing British troops’ ability against an enemy equipped with drones and electronic warfare. “The threats we face in the 21st century require an updated, modern approach to warfare – and our adaptable armed forces are preparing accordingly,” British defence minister Wes Streeting said in a statement.
Ukraine’s military has a “stable connection to Starlink” , the Ukrainian government said , 10 days after its ability to use the satellite communications system was suddenly restricted . But hours after the announcement, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, whose company owns Starlink, accused the Ukrainian military of violating ⁠the company’s terms. Neither Musk nor the US government, which operates Starshield using Starlink technology, took responsibility for imposing the curbs.
Bulgarians will vote ​in presidential elections on Sunday as Russia’s war in Ukraine spreads to the waters off Bulgaria’s Black Sea coast. Polls place acting president Iliana ⁠Iotova and ex-caretaker prime minister Andrey Gyurov as leaders in a race of 27 candidates. While the presidency is largely ceremonial, Sunday’s vote has taken ⁠on added significance after a series of drone attacks on merchant ships off Bulgaria’s Black Sea ​coast that likely killed about two-dozen international ‌seafarers.

### 17. ★★★☆☆ From Russian cages to Irish sanctuary: The five-year rescue journey of Bu and Tyapa

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T23:02:51+00:00
- **发生了什么**: For Bu and Tyapa, the five-year journey from Russia to the Irish countryside was anything but straightforward.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Tyapa (left) and Bu (right) were previously kept in small cages in the Republic of Dagestan in Russia
After years in cramped cages, two brown bears are adjusting to their new life following a remarkable rescue mission that spanned multiple countries, was disrupted by two major conflicts and involved more than 100 people.
For Bu and Tyapa, the five-year rescue journey from the Republic of Dagestan in Russia to the Irish countryside was anything but straightforward.
One bear had spent their life forced to perform in a circus, while the other was confined to a cage so small they could not stand upright.
Today, the bears are now adjusting to a totally different world among the trees and grass at Wild Ireland, a sanctuary for rescued animals in County Donegal.
Image caption, Tyapa is slowly settling into her new surroundings in Burnfoot, County Donegal
Wild Ireland founder Killian McLaughlin first learned about Bu and Tyapa in 2021 and committed to bringing them to safety in Donegal.
The sanctuary, located in Burnfoot, opened in October 2019 and provides a safe space for animals, including brown bears, monkeys, wolves, and lynx.
Image caption, Wild Ireland founder Killian McLaughlin says the bears&#x27; health improvements since their arrival have been remarkable
McLaughlin said the two bears were in extremely poor health when they were first rescued.
Former circus bear Bu had spent years confined to a small indoor cage after being forced to perform.
McLaughlin said Tyapa had lived in a cage measuring just 2.5m by 2.5m by 2m, which was too low for him to stand upright and had no roof, which left him exposed to the harsh winters and blistering summer heat.
The bear had no water dish and relied on a hose for drinking water. McLaughlin said he was being raised to be killed for an eventual taxidermy display.
When the bears were rescued, both were severely malnourished and showed signs of psychological trauma.
Blood tests also revealed serious kidney problems associated with prolonged dehydration.
Russia&#x27;s invasion of Ukraine in February 2022 made the original transport route through Ukraine impossible, said McLaughlin.
An alternative route through the Middle East was then explored, but plans were disrupted again in February 2026 when the escalating conflict involving Iran led to airspace closures and flight disruptions across the region.
More than 100 people were involved in the rescue operation, including volunteers, lawyers, veterinarians, animal transport specialists, cargo teams and officials in Russia, the United Arab Emirates and Ireland.
Image caption, The bears travelled from Moscow via Abu Dhabi to Dublin, arriving in the Republic of Ireland on 3 August 2026
In late October 2025, Dima and Yana Kim, who work at Tiger House, a sanctuary near St Petersburg that cares for former circus tigers, embarked on a 36-hour drive to Dagestan to rescue the bears.
Bu and Tyapa had to be cut out of their rusting steel cages, sedated and placed in secure transport crates for the journey back to Tiger House, where they received veterinary treatment and underwent quarantine.
After months of preparation and repeated delays, the bears eventually travelled from Moscow via Abu Dhabi to Dublin, arriving in Ireland in August.
Since arriving at Wild Ireland, McLaughlin said both bears have gained weight, their coats have improved, and follow-up blood tests have shown that their kidney function has returned to normal.
He said their habitat provides woodland, water and room to explore, as well as sheltered areas where they can retreat or move out of public view if they would like some privacy.
"These bears have suffered trauma and the first step of that recovery process is get them back out into the forest, get their paws into the soil and on the grass and get them exploring.
Working with bears is certainly not your everyday job, but for Wild Ireland team member Chloe McElhatton, it&#x27;s one she absolutely relishes.
Chloe has been working at Wild Ireland for just over a year. She studied zoology at Queen&#x27;s University Belfast, completing a four-year degree before joining the team.
"This is obviously a very unique job, and it&#x27;s quite different from what most people would do," she said.
Image caption, Chloe McElhatton is part of the bear team at Wild Ireland and says looking after bears is not your average 9-5 job
"We&#x27;ve done a lot of work hiding food around the bear habitats, encouraging them to search and forage for it themselves and this promotes their natural behaviours and helps them become more comfortable in that new open space," she said.
"Over time, we&#x27;ve seen their confidence build, and you can see great changes, both physically and mentally, in both of them."
Image caption, Liam, who is from France, says the two bears are big and impressive
Liam, who is from France, said the bears were one of his favourite animals to see when visiting the park.
"Very big and very impressive," is how he summed up Bu and Tyapa.
"I saw chickens, some wolves and some monkeys and then the bears which were definitely my favourite."
Image caption, Chris Roberts is a regular visitor to Wild Ireland and came to visit hoping to see a glimpse of the two new bears
Chris Roberts is from Manorcunningham, about 20 minutes from Wild Ireland, and has been visiting the site ever since it first opened in 2019.
"I&#x27;ve lost count of how many times we&#x27;ve been here, but we really wanted to come down today to see if we could catch a glimpse of Bu and Tyapa.
"We absolutely loved it and are delighted to see them doing so well, especially considering their backstory and the awful conditions they were once kept in."

</details>

QatarEnergy in Talks to Buy Up to 3 Million Tons of LNG From Woodside | Egypt Oil & Gas
QatarEnergy in Talks to Buy Up to 3 Million Tons of LNG From Woodside
Qatar’s state-owned energy company QatarEnergy is in talks to purchase up to 3 million metric tons per year (mtpa) of liquefied natural gas (LNG) from Australia's Woodside Energy's Louisiana LNG export facility in the US under a potential long-term supply agreement, Reuters reported, citing three people familiar with the negotiations.
If finalized, the deal could cover supplies for 15 to 20 years from the 16.5 mtpa facility under construction, according to one of the sources. Woodside declined to comment on market speculation, while QatarEnergy had not immediately responded to Reuters' request for comment.
The negotiations come as QatarEnergy seeks to secure supplies for some of its customers through the US spot market amid disruptions to shipping through the Strait of Hormuz. The conflict involving the US, Israel, and Iran has curtailed maritime traffic through the strategic waterway, Reuters reported.
QatarEnergy has also expanded its potential exposure to US LNG exports through its 70% ownership stake in the Golden Pass LNG project in Louisiana, which is designed to export up to 18 mtpa once completed.
The latest talks follow a long-term supply agreement announced by US-based Cheniere Energy on October 22 to supply QatarEnergy with 4 mtpa of LNG. Under the agreement, 2 mtpa will come from Cheniere's Sabine Pass facility in Louisiana, while the remaining 2 mtpa will be sourced from its Corpus Christi terminal in Texas.
Reuters also reported that QatarEnergy was in advanced talks with US LNG exporter Venture Global to purchase at least 3 mtpa under a potential long-term agreement.
Woodside's Louisiana LNG and Venture Global are among the US suppliers with the largest amounts of uncommitted LNG capacity, according to Reuters. Louisiana LNG is reportedly seeking liquefaction fees exceeding $2.50 per million British thermal units (MMBtu), above average US market prices, in exchange for offering buyers greater contractual flexibility and potentially shorter contract terms.

### 21. ★★★☆☆ America’s farmers are flashing warning signals about inflation, and the Fed is paying attention. Protect yourself now - AOL.com

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融

This article adheres to strict editorial standards. Some or all links may be monetized.
For anybody worried about inflation in 2026, the next big warning might not come from Wall Street or Washington. It could be coming from America’s farms, and the Federal Reserve is listening.
Austan Goolsbee, president and CEO of the Federal Reserve Bank of Chicago, recently traveled to rural Illinois to gather what one farmer called a “boots-on-the-ground” perspective. The trip was featured (1) in a Bloomberg Television report on Oct. 17.
Jeff Bezos backs a platform that lets anyone invest in rental homes for as little as $100 — 6 ways to build wealth like a landlord without actually being one
Dave Ramsey warns nearly 50% of Americans are making 1 big Social Security mistake.  Here’s what it is and 3 simple steps to fix it ASAP
A record 45% of central banks plan to grow gold reserves — and many investors are following suit. Get your free gold IRA guide from Priority Gold
One farmer featured in the report was Troy Uphoff, whose family has been farming in the Federal Reserve’s 7th District since 1842. When Bloomberg’s Michael McKee asked whether farmers have had a rough two years, Uphoff answered, “Absolutely.”
He said crop prices have been lower, mainly because of tariffs. At the same time, the cost of running a farm keeps going up.
“Diesel fuel right now is roughly 80% higher than it was one year ago,” Uphoff said.
He added that seed and fertilizer prices have also climbed. In Uphoff’s view, costs have increased faster than crop prices, leaving many growers with little profit over the past two years.
During the Bloomberg report, Goolsbee said talking to people on the ground can help identify trends before they show up in the data. He said the message he heard across many industries was “nothing about demand drying up, all about cost pressures.”
He pointed to the cost of shipping, fertilizer and diesel as examples.
Goolsbee called this problem “the nightmare of the central bank,” explaining that when the economy faces stagflationary shocks, there’s no “obvious playbook.”
Economists use the term stagflation when prices rise while the economy slows down and unemployment climbs.
Goolsbee also warned that recent shocks, from COVID-19-era supply issues to tariffs and higher oil prices, have lasted longer than expected. Inflation in the U.S. has been above the Fed’s 2% target for more than five years, he noted.
“I do think even though the Fed can’t pump oil, it can’t ignore permanent inflation shocks that threaten to drive inflation above the target,” he said.
In September, the Consumer Price Index (2) (CPI), a key measure of inflation, rose 3.7% over the last 12 months, according to the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS). That was up from 3.4% in August.
The Fed has already started making moves to try to get inflation back to 2%, announcing in a Sept. 16 statement (3) that it was raising its benchmark interest rate by a quarter of a percentage point to between 3.75% and 4.00%. It was the first hike since mid-2023.
The reason? “Inflation remains elevated,” said the statement.
And it might not be the last move the Fed makes in 2026. The Federal Open Market Committee, which is responsible for adjusting interest rates, is set to meet twice before the end of the year: on Oct. 27-28 and Dec. 8-9.
Whether they plan to adjust interest rates again remains to be seen. However, according to the CME Group’s FedWatch tool (4), which tracks probabilities of a rate change by looking at 30-Day Federal Funds futures prices, the market expects the Fed to keep rates unchanged at its October meeting — but raise them by another quarter of a percentage point in December.
That would take the benchmark rate to a range of 4.00% to 4.25%.
American farmers may be feeling the worst of it for now, but that could change. Higher diesel and gasoline prices don’t just hit businesses, they hit household budgets.
In the latest inflation report, for instance, the BLS said gasoline, which rose by 9.1% in September, accounted for more than half of the month’s rise in consumer prices.
Meanwhile, the Bloomberg report noted that rising interest rates could make borrowing more expensive for farmers already dealing with higher costs. The same could be true for households.
In short, the combination of rising inflation and higher interest rates could result in households facing a “double whammy” of cost-of-living increases.
Here are a few ways Americans are looking to protect their money.
Gold has long been seen as a store of value in times of inflation and uncertainty. That’s because, unlike fiat currencies, the precious yellow metal can’t be created out of thin air or printed at will by central banks. This inherently limited supply can help it store value in the face of inflation.
Gold is also considered the ultimate safe haven. Since it’s not tied to any one country, currency or economy, investors often flock to it — driving prices higher — in times of economic uncertainty or geopolitical instability.
In the current investing environment, the appeal of gold hasn’t been lost on experts.
Ray Dalio, founder of Bridgewater Associates, one of the world’s largest hedge funds, suggests investors should allocate as much as 15% of their portfolio (5) in gold.
But what’s the best way to add gold to a retirement portfolio? If you’re curious about adding precious metals to your broader inflation-hedging strategy, a gold IRA from Goldco lets you hold physical gold and other metals while still getting the tax advantages of an IRA.
Goldco is widely regarded as one of the leading companies in the space, with a 4.8/5 rating on Trustpilot and an A+ from the Better Business Bureau. When inflation and interest rates rise together, stocks and bonds tend to become volatile. This is why it might be a smart idea to diversify your portfolio by investing in alternative assets.

Investing doesn’t have to be complicated. Many people simply want a simpler way to grow their money, stay on track for retirement and avoid constantly tinkering with their portfolio.
That’s why some investors might want to leave it to professionals.
If you prefer a hands-off, tech-forward approach to building wealth, Vanguard’s Digital Advisor puts the investing expertise of one of the world’s largest asset managers right at your fingertips.

Hundreds of thousands march in France as protests take place across Europe – as it happened
French police have made 1,000 arrests and Italian officers have raided homes in Milan, with protests also taking place in Spain and Albania
Patrick Daly (now); Marina Dunbar , Frances Mao and Emily Mackay (earlier)
Sat 24 Oct 2026 16.22 EDT First published on Sat 24 Oct 2026 04.14 EDT
Paris protesters report heavy handed response from police
Protesters try to force entry into Colosseum in Rome
French interior minister: 1,000 arrested during protests, with number expected to rise
25,000 people rally in Madrid for pension guarantees
Are French high school buildings dilapidated and rat-infested with collapsing ceilings?

French police make 135 arrests, interior minister says

Paris protest route moved away from Bastille and pushed back to afternoon
Some protest bans overturned in last-minute court challenges

Protesters marching in Paris. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters
Protesters marching in Paris. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters
French police arrested almost 1,000 people on Saturday during the protests held across the country, interior minister Laurent Nunez said.
He told reporters that the number “will rise” as more data from police becomes available.
Although marches over the cost of living “passed off well” in cities, officers found themselves later confronted with “an extremely elevated level of violence” from “ultra-left” groups, Nunez said.
France's interior minister Laurent Nunez has given an update on the number of arrests at Saturday’s protests Photograph: Julien de Rosa/EPA
Paris protesters report heavy handed response from police
