### 38. ★★★☆☆ This Stock Market Metric Warns History May Be Repeating Itself. Here's How the Smartest Investors Are Preparing. - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T07:18:01+00:00
- **发生了什么**: This Stock Market Metric Warns History May Be Repeating Itself. Here's How the Smartest Investors Are Preparing.&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

After years of record-breaking growth, major market indexes have stagnated. The S&P 500 ( ^GSPC -0.52% ) and the Dow Jones Industrial Average ( ^DJI -1.23% ) have each dipped by around 2% from their all-time highs, while the Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC -0.21% ) has slipped by close to 6%.
Investors are also feeling conflicted about the future of the market. Nearly 46% of investors believe stock prices will fall over the next six months, according to the latest weekly survey from the American Association of Individual Investors, while 37% are bullish about the future, and the remaining 17% expect little change.
While it&#x27;s impossible to predict how stocks will perform in the coming months, the market is sending a warning signal that has only been seen during the dot-com bubble. Here&#x27;s how investors can prepare right now.
In the late 1990s, tech stocks were booming as excitement about the internet reached fever pitch. Many tech companies saw record-breaking initial public offerings (IPOs), reaching valuations that were astronomical for the time. Between 1995 and 1999 alone, the S&P 500 soared by close to 200%.
Another metric that was reaching record highs, though, was the S&P 500 Shiller cyclically adjusted price-to-earnings (CAPE) ratio. This ratio measures the S&P 500&#x27;s long-term valuation, with higher figures suggesting that the index is overvalued.
Historically, higher ratios have been followed by a drop in stock prices, and this metric has predicted market crashes such as the 1929 Great Depression. In early 1999, the ratio surpassed 40 for the first time in history as the dot-com bubble grew. Just over a year later, in March 2000, the bear market officially began.
Now the CAPE ratio is elevated again. The metric surpassed 40 in May of this year, where it&#x27;s remained ever since. This is only the second time in history that it&#x27;s stayed this high for months at a time.
To be clear, this doesn&#x27;t necessarily mean we&#x27;re headed toward a dot-com-style crash. This metric provides a snapshot of the market&#x27;s valuation over time, but it can&#x27;t predict when the next bear market will begin, how severe it might be, or how long it might last.
That said, it does suggest that stocks are incredibly richly valued, making this the priciest market in decades.
If a recession or stock market crash is coming, the best move investors can make is to buy quality stocks and hold them for the long term.
Companies with weak foundations will have a harder time rebounding from downturns, and some may not recover at all. Countless tech stocks soared to record valuations in the 1990s, for example, only to go bankrupt just a few years later when the bubble popped. The tech stocks that survived were those with healthy finances, a durable competitive advantage, and capable leadership teams.
Even healthy companies can experience severe short-term volatility, which is why a long-term outlook is key. Microsoft , for example, plunged by 60% during the dot-com bear market. Apple lost more than 51% of its value in a single trading day in 2000. And Amazon sank by a staggering 94% between 1999 and 2001.
Now, all of these stocks are industry-leading giants, and the investors who reaped the greatest rewards were those who held on and continued investing even during the bleakest moments.
No two bear markets are identical, but even if history is repeating itself and we&#x27;re staring down another market crash, the smartest investors aren&#x27;t panicking. Rather, they&#x27;re loading up on healthy stocks with robust fundamentals , and they&#x27;re giving those investments plenty of time to generate life-changing wealth.
In the wise words of Warren Buffett: "Keep buying it through thick and thin, and especially through thin."

### 39. ★★★☆☆ If the Stock Market Crashes, This Is the First ETF I'm Loading Up On - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T11:50:46+00:00
- **发生了什么**: If the Stock Market Crashes, This Is the First ETF I'm Loading Up On&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The stock market will crash again at some point. There have already been numerous boom-and-bust cycles throughout the stock market&#x27;s history, making the next crash more a matter of when than if.
Unfortunately, nobody actually knows when it will happen. Therefore, the best way to defend against a stock market crash is to have a plan for when the time comes. When it does, the Invesco QQQ Trust ( QQQ -0.01% ) could be the first exchange-traded fund (ETF) I load up on.
Artificial intelligence (AI) has driven the stock market higher for several years now. It&#x27;s not a fluke; AI has become arguably the most important technology in decades.
The Invesco QQQ offers remarkably broad exposure to most companies involved in the AI trade. The ETF tracks the Nasdaq-100 , an index filled with the world&#x27;s most prominent technology companies.
Of course, that means a heavy lean into AI. Investors will find Nvidia , Microsoft , Amazon , Micron Technology , Meta Platforms , Alphabet , and Tesla among the ETF&#x27;s top holdings. That covers GPUs and memory for data centers, the hyperscalers, and even emerging industries, such as autonomous driving and robotics.
Why the next crash will probably create jaw-dropping buying opportunities
At the same time, excitement for AI has propelled the Nasdaq-100 and the Invesco QQQ. The ETF is up a whopping 98% over the past three years alone. Today, the stocks in the Nasdaq-100 trade at a blended price-to-earnings ratio of approximately 30. That&#x27;s already a lofty valuation, and what if the AI boom slows and earnings deteriorate? That certainly wouldn&#x27;t be good.
Tech stocks tend to outperform other sectors in bull markets. But that also means they tend to fall further during market downturns.
You can see how the QQQ has plunged during major market crashes, for instance, falling 83% following the burst of the dot-com market bubble in the early 2000s and about 50% during the financial crisis later that decade.
I can&#x27;t say with certainty that the AI craze will trigger a market crash. That said, there are risks .
The leading frontier model companies, such as OpenAI and Anthropic, are chasing massive market valuations while still logging hefty financial losses. It could become a big problem for the market if these AI companies continue to burn so much capital.
Without profits, companies, shareholders, and investors will only tolerate their enormous spending budgets for so long. If data center projects slow, the entire AI trade could unwind. But even if there is an eventual market crash, it doesn&#x27;t mean that the technology itself is doomed.
Remember, the internet caused a bubble and eventual crash before ultimately becoming a foundational pillar of the modern global economy. Perhaps AI could follow a similar path. If it does, the Invesco QQQ is a fantastic way to invest in the eventual recovery.

### 40. ★★★☆☆ Energy Transfer vs. Kinder Morgan: Which Natural Gas Pipeline Stock Is the Better Buy for the AI Power Boom? - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🛢️ 能源
- **来源**: GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T18:35:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Energy Transfer vs. Kinder Morgan: Which Natural Gas Pipeline Stock Is the Better Buy for the AI Power Boom?&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Energy Transfer vs. Kinder Morgan: Which Natural Gas Pipeline Stock Is the Better Buy for the AI Power Boom?
Energy Transfer (TXSE: ET) and Kinder Morgan (NYSE: KMI) are two of the largest midstream businesses in North America. Although both have cut their dividends in the past, they are growing them at a steady clip today. However, as artificial intelligence (AI) shifts the demand story in the electricity sector, Kinder Morgan is likely the better-positioned business, if you're willing to pay for it. Here's what you need to know to choose between these two midstream operators.
There is a massive effort underway to build the infrastructure that supports artificial intelligence . However, at its core, AI is just a fancy computer program. It doesn't exist without electricity. The more AI infrastructure that's built, the more electricity is needed. Between 2024 and 2030, the International Energy Agency expects electricity demand from AI to increase "more than four times faster than the growth of total electricity consumption from all other sectors."
Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again. In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. For the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. Continue »
Image source: Getty Images. The world needs more electricity in a big way, with some estimates that electricity demand will grow by 60% between 2025 and 2045. That's up from growth of just 10% between 2005 and 2025. Pipeline companies are a vital part of supplying this electricity because natural gas is increasingly being used to generate power.
There's more good news. Businesses like Energy Transfer and Kinder Morgan largely charge fees for the use of their energy infrastructure assets. So while commodity prices can be highly volatile, midstream companies largely sidestep that risk. The volume flowing through their systems is more important than the price of what is being moved. In other words, Energy Transfer and Kinder Morgan are likely to benefit from AI regardless of what happens to natural gas prices.
Which is better positioned: Energy Transfer or Kinder Morgan?
There is a notable difference between Energy Transfer and Kinder Morgan regarding natural gas. Natural gas makes up around 35% of Energy Transfer's business, while Kinder Morgan's exposure is 67%. In fact, Kinder Morgan claims to own the largest natural gas transmission network in the United States.
From this perspective, Kinder Morgan is better positioned to benefit from increasing demand for the natural gas used to generate electricity. If that is all you are focused on, it is probably the better choice for your portfolio.
The problem is that Kinder Morgan's dividend yield is only 3.4%, which is far below the 6.7% yield that you could collect from Energy Transfer. To buy either for a dividend portfolio, you need to accept that both have both cut their dividends in the past. They are growing again, as noted above. In fact, slow and steady dividend growth is likely to be the norm for each at this point. Still, if income is your real focus, then Energy Transfer is the obvious winner. And, while it may not benefit as much as Kinder Morgan from AI's voracious appetite for electricity, it will still benefit.
The simple answer here is that Kinder Morgan is more leveraged to natural gas than Energy Transfer. But that's looking at the comparison in something of a vacuum. Wall Street clearly knows Kinder Morgan is tied into the AI story, and it has been priced accordingly. That has left the stock with a relatively low yield in a sector known for providing attractive yields. In other words, if you are trying to live off of the income your portfolio generates, you may be better off with less natural gas exposure and more yield. And that could make Energy Transfer the more appropriate choice.
Before you buy stock in Energy Transfer, consider this:

### 42. ★★★☆☆ Two rescued after Nairobi building collapse with five still missing

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T17:25:46+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Police have launched a manhunt for the building's owner and contractor after the multi-storey building collapsed while still under construction.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Two people have been pulled alive from the rubble of a building that collapsed in Kenya&#x27;s capital, Nairobi, as a search continues for five still missing.
Dramatic footage shows the bottom floor of the block, thought to have been 16 or 18 storeys, giving way before the rest of the building crumples, sending a plume of dust into the surrounding streets on Friday.
Dozens of builders had been working on the upper floors when it collapsed, but managed to emerge largely unscathed. Those missing were thought to have been in shops at the base of the building.
The incident has raised fresh concerns about construction standards and regulations in Kenya.
Police said on Saturday that a manhunt had been launched for the building&#x27;s owner and the contractor responsible for the site.
The block was under construction when it collapsed on Friday afternoon, with footage showing scaffolding still wrapped around the outside of the building. Officials said 11 shops had already opened on the bottom floors.
Interior ministry official Kipchumba Murkomen said 66 of the 67 construction workers in the building at the time had been able to get out unharmed, while the other had suffered injuries that were not life-threatening.
He had said on Friday that seven people had been reported missing and rescue teams had made contact with some of those trapped beneath the rubble.
The two survivors rescued on Saturday have been taken to hospital, police said.
Images from the scene, in Nairobi&#x27;s Eastleigh area, show the concrete floors of the building piled on top of one another, as rescue workers saw through metal to get to those believed to have been stuck underneath.
Image caption, Footage taken from nearby shows moments before (left) and moments after (right) the building collapsed
Questions have emerged about why the building suddenly collapsed and whether it met planning regulations.
Kenya&#x27;s National Construction Authority said in a statement that it had identified the company behind the development, as well as the architect and structural engineer, but did not say whether the site had been inspected before Friday.
The National Police Service said it had launched an investigation to establish the cause of the collapse and determine whether anyone was criminally liable.
It warned that anyone found responsible for negligence or violating building regulations would "face the full force of the law".
Nairobi Governor Sakaja Johnson told local media that the owners of the building did not have the required paperwork for the building to be occupied.
He urged the national government to give local authorities greater powers to penalise "developers who disregard approved building plans and safety standards".
Building collapses are not uncommon in Nairobi, with poor construction or improper planning often cited as contributing factors.

</details>

A man inspects damage at the site of an airstrike, reportedly carried out by Saudi Arabia, that targeted the Asr district of Yemen's Houthi-held capital Sanaa on October 24, 2026 [Mohammed Huwais/AFP]
Saudi Arabia has intercepted drones and missiles over its capital, Riyadh, and southern areas, the Saudi-led coalition has said, as fighting with Yemen’s Houthis continues.
Coalition spokesman Major-General Turki al-Maliki said on Sunday its forces intercepted and destroyed a Houthi ballistic missile launched towards the capital city that morning, as well as a drone in the Riyadh region, and a cruise missile aimed at the southwestern city of Khamis Mushait, with explosions ringing out in the Saudi capital.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 Time ‘running out’ to reach Yemen’s Taiz amid deepening crisis, UN says
list 2 of 4 Hospital in Yemen’s Taiz warns of collapse in days because of Houthi siege
list 3 of 4 Iran says won’t allow US warships to get close as Trump ponders new attacks
list 4 of 4 Houthis launch attacks on Saudi Arabia and Yemen’s Aden
It later reported the interception of 10 ballistic missiles fired towards southern ‌Saudi Arabia.
The attacks come as operations resumed at two terminals at Saudi Arabia’s King Khalid international airport after deadly attacks earlier this month by the Houthis, an armed group also known as Ansar Allah (Arabic for Supporters of God).
Operations at the airport were halted on October 10 after 13 people were killed and more than 300 others were wounded in a Houthi attack .
Late on Saturday, Riyadh airport said in a statement that international flights would resume from Terminal 2 on Sunday, while Terminal 1 would be designated for domestic flights.
Houthi military spokesman Yahya Saree warned that the airport was still “unsafe” and a “military target” for the Iran-backed group.
“The Yemeni Armed Forces warn all international, regional and local airlines – as well as staff and travellers – against using King Khalid airport in Riyadh, as it is unsafe and serves as a military target for our forces,” he said. “The Saudi regime shall bear the consequences of any operations at the airport.”
The Iran-aligned Houthis on Saturday said they targeted three Saudi airports, including one in Riyadh, energy installations, a pipeline and an airbase.
The Saudi civil defence agency said it also responded to falling debris in the Mecca and Medina regions on Saturday after intercepting two Houthi missiles.
The Houthis denied targeting Mecca and Medina, two of the holiest cities in Islam, calling the Saudi claim a “huge lie”.
In Yemen on Sunday, the Houthis advanced on Jabal Sabr, the strategic mountain overlooking the city of Taiz, which has become the focus of the fighting between the Houthis and Yemen’s government forces.
Houthi fighters have besieged the city and cut off the main road to Aden, which serves as the interim capital of Yemen’s internationally recognised government.
The United Nations has warned that “time is running out” to deliver aid to the 800,000 people trapped in the city.
Yemen’s government forces, meanwhile, said they conducted more than 700 operations against Houthi targets on Saturday, claiming to have killed more than 300 fighters.
Fighting between government forces and Houthi fighters has intensified across multiple fronts in Yemen in recent weeks, causing a spiralling humanitarian crisis .
The war in Yemen escalated sharply when the Houthis seized strategic territory from forces loyal to the country’s internationally recognised government along the Red Sea coast last month.
The Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabia, which is aligned with Yemen’s government forces, have dealt a huge blow to the kingdom, the world’s biggest exporter of crude oil, disrupting its businesses, airlines and normal life, and damaging its international image.

### 48. ★★★☆☆ Man arrested after fire at Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T15:21:44+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Some 200 emergency personnel were deployed to fight the blaze at the Nazi dictator's Alpine residence in Germany.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch as fire engulfs Hitler&#x27;s mountaintop retreat
Police in the southern German state of Bavaria have detained a 33-year-old German man during a fire at Adolf Hitler&#x27;s Alpine mountain retreat, the Kehlsteinhaus, also known as the Eagle&#x27;s Nest.
Dispatchers in Traunstein received a fire alarm from the building at 21:15 local time (19:15 GMT) on Friday and firefighters found it engulfed in flames.
Some 200 emergency personnel were deployed to the scene where the blaze was still burning early on Saturday, as police said difficult terrain hindered efforts to bring the fire under control.
Police said they apprehended the man riding a motorcycle near the scene, German media reported. A criminal investigation has been launched.
There is currently nothing to suggest the involvement of further suspects, police official Gerhart Holzinger told a press conference on Saturday afternoon.
Public prosecutor Rainer Vietze said a psychiatric report had concluded the 33-year-old man showed signs of possible mental illness and diminished responsibility might therefore be assumed.
Vietze said a possible political motive was also being considered, but stressed the investigation was in its early stages.
The residence, above the town of Berchtesgaden, was one of several buildings in the area used by the Nazi dictator and his entourage during World War Two.
Hitler and top Nazi officials used his nearby Berghof residence to plan the invasion of the USSR in 1941 and the whole complex was considered one of his headquarters.
He also used the area as a backdrop for propaganda films portraying him as a lover of animals and nature.
Local media reported that site had been "largely destroyed" and the public was urged to avoid the area.
Bavaria&#x27;s Interior Minister Joachim Herrmann told reporters that the site would "under no circumstances remain a ruin", broadcaster BR24 reported. "It is important that we continue our work of remembrance," he added.
Image caption, The retreat was seen smouldering on Saturday morning
Completed by the Nazis in 1938, the Eagle&#x27;s Nest, which sits at an elevation of around 1,800m (5,900ft), escaped destruction after their defeat in 1945.
Since then, it has become a popular tourist destination and houses a mountain inn.
It serves as "a cautionary reminder of the abysses of the inhumane Nazi dictatorship", according to its official website , external .
During WW2, Hitler and his followers were responsible for the deaths of tens of millions of people, including the mass murder of European Jews. Homosexuals, gypsies, the disabled and ethnic minorities were sent to concentration camps and put to death.
There has been a wider debate in Germany and Vienna over whether to preserve, repurpose or demolish buildings connected to Hitler.
While some say these buildings should be preserved for historical reasons, others have raised concerns that they could be used as monuments or places of pilgrimage for neo-Nazis and far-right extremists.
In June, Berlin authorities announced they would tear down one of Hitler&#x27;s bunkers - described at the time as "the power centre of Nazi Germany" - to build flats and offices on the site.
Those critical of the plans said the bunker had "significant historical value" and that it should have been turned into a museum and memorial site with an exhibit about the end of the war.
And in July, after years of controversy, Austrian authorities refurbished the house in the town of Braunau am Inn in which Hitler was born and turned it into a police station.
Police station opens at Hitler&#x27;s birthplace in bid to rid site of Nazi link
&#x27;Absolute madness&#x27;: Row over plan to demolish Nazi bunker under Berlin

Why a far-right cabinet minister in Israel has Netanyahu's ear
Why a far-right cabinet minister in Israel has Netanyahu's ear

Israel's most far-right cabinet minister helped dictate hardline policies toward Palestinians in recent years. Benjamin Netanyahu vows to give him a senior position if Netanyahu is reelected next week.
SACHA PFEIFFER, HOST: We've spent much of this program talking about the big issues weighing on the minds of people in Wisconsin and across the country ahead of the midterm elections this fall. But on the other side of the globe, there's another hotly contested election coming up in just a few days. On Tuesday, voters in Israel will determine if Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu will stay in power for another term. The race is razor-thin, and if he wins, he's promising to share power with Israel's most extremist political figure. NPR's Daniel Estrin reports from Tel Aviv. (CHEERING) DANIEL ESTRIN, BYLINE: Itamar Ben-Gvir takes the stage at a campaign stop in Tel Aviv and exclaims, the people of Israel live. (SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED RECORDING) ITAMAR BEN-GVIR: (Speaking Hebrew). ESTRIN: He's Israel's national security minister. At this event for English-speaking immigrants, he's brought a simultaneous translator. He outlines what he's done these past few years of war. He drastically limited the food fed to Palestinian prisoners in Israeli jails. (SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED RECORDING) BEN-GVIR: (Through interpreter) They used to come in skinny and come out fat. ESTRIN: But now... (SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED RECORDING) BEN-GVIR: (Through interpreter) They come in fat, and they come out skinny. ESTRIN: He tells the crowd about one meeting when officials were trying to comply with a nutritional requirement to provide prisoners with fruit. One official suggested a lemon. Ben-Gvir said no because lemon juice would make prisoners' food taste better. NOA SATTATH: During a time of war, he's busy with the lemon. ESTRIN: Noa Sattath of the Association for Civil Rights in Israel - the group argued in court that prisoners were being starved and tortured. The Supreme Court overturned the policy. SATTATH: And so this is, like, the level of brutality and cruelty that Ben-Gvir has been exemplifying all along. ESTRIN: Ben-Gvir wears a lapel pin in the shape of a noose to celebrate his victory legislating the death penalty by hanging for Palestinians convicted of attacking Israelis. He also relaxed gun laws, doubling the number of gun licenses for Israeli civilians. One man in the crowd who gives his first name, Zion, says he's grateful to be able to have a gun to protect himself after the Hamas-led attacks of October 7, 2023. ZION: I didn't finish the army, OK? I couldn't have a weapon permit. Recently, Ben-Gvir made it possible for me to have a permit for a weapon. Told myself, who did something for you that you can say, oh, he did it for me? It's Ben-Gvir. That's it. ESTRIN: Ben-Gvir used to be on the margins of Israeli politics. He was convicted two decades ago for supporting an Israeli party outlawed for being too extremist. YOSSI MEKELBERG: Ben-Gvir was supposed to be an anecdote in Israeli politics. No one took him seriously. ESTRIN: Yossi Mekelberg of the Chatham House think tank says Netanyahu relied on Ben-Gvir to form a coalition because so many other politicians refused to share power with Netanyahu because he's on trial for corruption. MEKELBERG: By sheer accident of history of Netanyahu's corruption trial, he became one of the most powerful politicians. ESTRIN: He believes Ben-Gvir helped dictate Israel's hard-line policies these past few years, prolonging the Gaza War and emboldening extremist Israeli settlers in the occupied West Bank. MEKELBERG: So he became, in many ways, the face of Israel in much of the international media. It's not a very pretty face. ESTRIN: This spring, Ben-Gvir posted videos of himself taunting international activists who were arrested while sailing toward Gaza. (SOUNDBITE OF ARCHIVED RECORDING) BEN-GVIR: (Speaking Hebrew). ESTRIN: Italy and France objected, and Netanyahu said Ben-Gvir wasn't acting in line with Israel's values. But now Netanyahu is trying to appease far-right voters. He says he'll give Ben-Gvir a senior position in his government if he wins elections next week. Daniel Estrin, NPR News, Tel Aviv.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

WANA (Oct 25) &#8211; Oil prices settled at $116 a barrel on the previous day, with the possibility of climbing further and surpassing $120. Meanwhile, U.S. officials continue to insist that the Strait of Hormuz remains open. Yet images and reports from the region, where fires at oil facilities and tankers have become an almost daily occurrence, tell a different story. Oil prices reinforce the same message. What the White House is trying to promote amounts to a blatant denial of reality.
The scale of a person or a country's stature can be judged by the scope of its actions. Through the war he launched against Iran and its continuation, U.S. President Donald Trump has brought his country to a point where, despite all the resources of a superpower, it is reduced to taking small-scale measures.
On Friday, the U.S. Department of Energy ordered the emergency delivery of up to 400,000 barrels of crude oil to Phillips 66's Lake Charles refinery in response to temporary disruptions in crude oil deliveries. The refinery has a processing capacity of approximately 264,000 barrels per day, meaning the released volume is equivalent to around one and a half days of its consumption.
Trump has also reached agreements with the presidents of France and Russia to secure diesel exports to the United States. Compared with U.S. consumption, the volume involved is equivalent to only three or four days' worth of demand. Remarkably, the U.S. president has reduced his country's high-level relationship with Russia from strategic issues to a matter of a few million liters of diesel, even as the war in Ukraine continues.
A superpower that once negotiated massive contracts and pursued large-scale initiatives, and whose president spoke of territorial expansion early in his administration, has now been reduced to a diesel beggar and a purveyor of increasingly implausible statements.
An Iranian newspaper with a cover photo of a caricature depicting U.S. President Donald Trump, in Tehran, Iran, May 5, 2026. Majid Asgaripour/WANA (West Asia News Agency)
The following points help illustrate the damage that high energy prices are inflicting on the United States and its president.
The Trump administration brought Caracas to the point of submission through a naval blockade, followed by what it presented as a decisive blow: the capture of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro. Washington also threatened other Venezuelan leaders with a similar fate.
Trump promoted the campaign as a way to reduce drug trafficking into the United States and, of course, gain control over Venezuela's oil resources. With oil now at $116 a barrel, compared with $65 before the war—a 78 percent increase—the strategic value of gaining control over Venezuelan oil resources has become far less meaningful. The Venezuelan operation had lasted approximately five months.
The deployment of three U.S. destroyers on August 18, 2025, marked the beginning of the naval pressure campaign against Venezuela. The stated objective was to combat narcoterrorism and violent drug cartels, while the operation also involved the use of lethal force. During this period, targeting small vessels operating in the Caribbean helped create the impression of a blockade, disrupted small-scale maritime activities and cast an increasingly threatening shadow over Venezuela. Over time, the pressure expanded to oil tankers and, eventually, vessels more broadly.
On December 16, 2025, Trump announced a blockade targeting sanctioned oil tankers. This marked a major escalation and brought the confrontation closer to a full-scale maritime blockade. On December 25, 2025, according to U.S. officials, the restrictions on sanctioned tankers had evolved into an oil quarantine, effectively amounting to a comprehensive energy blockade.
To exert pressure on Venezuela, the United States deployed an aircraft carrier, 11 warships, 12 F-35 fighter jets and 15,000 personnel in and around the Caribbean.
All these deployments, the extensive publicity surrounding action against a longstanding adversary, and the government's and media's sustained focus on Venezuela amount to little more than wasted effort for American citizens when oil prices remain so high. Before the war against Iran began—or before its stalemate became apparent—the campaign against Caracas had provided Trump with a positive achievement he could present to voters. Rising energy prices have substantially undermined that political advantage.
Did Trump Just Reveal the US Plan for Iran’s Oil? ‘Like Venezuela’
WANA (Sep 14) &#8211; Donald Trump’s latest remarks about the future of the U.S. presence in Iran have added a significant new dimension to the debate over the war: Oil is one of Washington’s calculations for the post-war period. Speaking during his visit to Ireland on Sunday, the U.S. president was asked whether the [&hellip;]
2. Inflation, Treasury Yields and Household Borrowing Costs
The war and the resulting rise in energy prices have created significant problems for government borrowing costs and the interest rates paid by American households.
Governments generally have two main ways to finance budget deficits. The first is to create money, which can fuel inflation. The second is to borrow by issuing bonds, through which governments raise funds from investors, including companies and other governments, and pay interest according to an agreed schedule.
Money creation can generate greater inflationary pressure, while borrowing, when managed with fiscal discipline, generally has a more limited inflationary effect. However, when market expectations point to higher inflation in the future, investors demand higher yields in exchange for lending money to the government.
Since the war began, yields on 10-year U.S. Treasury bonds have risen by approximately half a percentage point. Estimates suggest that, if this increase persists, the U.S. government will have to pay an additional $8 billion in interest this year and $30 billion in 2027.
This comes as the government is already expected to spend around $1 trillion this year—equivalent to 14 percent of the federal budget—on interest payments. If yields rise sharply, borrowing costs will increase further.
This is only one aspect of the pressure on the bond market. High energy prices also reduce the capacity of governments and companies to purchase government bonds because they must devote more of their resources to energy expenses. This does not necessarily mean they will stop buying bonds. Instead, the U.S. government may have to offer higher yields to make its debt more attractive to investors.
Yet the more important issue is not simply the increase in government borrowing costs. It is the effect of higher interest rates on the loans available to ordinary citizens. The impact on mortgage, auto-loan and credit-card payments has already reached an estimated $300 to $400 per month for a typical household.
Given the delicate balance between household expenses, which leaves many families with little or no money to save, this additional burden is substantial. Beyond the difficulties facing the government, rising household costs are also making it harder for Trump and the Republican Party to attract voters.
For now, Trump's primary concern is therefore not necessarily the effect of higher interest rates on government borrowing. It is the impact on household loan payments and the political consequences that could follow at the ballot box.
People walk near a billboard depicting U.S. President Donald Trump on a building in Tehran, Iran, July 27, 2026. Majid Asgaripour/WANA (West Asia News Agency)
3. The Myth of America's Windfall From High Energy Prices
Some individuals and companies in the United States benefit from high energy prices, but those gains are far from universal. Private oil and gas producers can earn substantial profits by selling their output at higher prices. Domestically, however, much of this increased revenue comes at the expense of consumers who must pay more.
Households face higher bills, more expensive gasoline and increased transportation costs for goods. These pressures can weaken support for Trump and the Republican Party.
Consequently, U.S. oil and gas export shipments cannot, by themselves, be presented as evidence of major achievements for Washington or Trump. Politically, rising oil prices undermine the electoral position of the party in power.
Diesel is particularly important to transportation and a broad range of commercial activities. It is widely used in industry, agriculture, shipping and other sectors of the economy. When diesel prices rise, companies face tighter cash flow and higher operating costs.
To cope, businesses may have to scale back their operations, raise the prices of their goods and services—risking a loss of customers—or borrow money to cover their expenses.
These choices place considerable psychological and financial pressure on business owners as they struggle to keep their operations running, ultimately reducing their profits.
WANA (Oct 24) &#8211; Oil prices settled at their highest level in nearly six months on Friday, October 23, amid escalating tensions in the Middle East and growing concerns over potential disruptions to energy supplies. Meanwhile, major US stock indexes closed lower, while European markets also mostly declined. Oil Prices Rise in Global Markets [&hellip;]
The government does not benefit directly from higher energy prices in the same way producers do. Instead, it can collect additional tax revenue from the increased profits of major energy companies.
That said, Republican administrations generally favor lower taxes and, correspondingly, lower spending on social programs. From the Republican perspective, heavily taxing large companies penalizes entrepreneurs. The argument is that successful business owners should be allowed to reinvest their earnings in expansion, thereby creating jobs, generating wealth and benefiting the country.
Even under ordinary tax rates, however, the government can receive additional revenue when energy companies' profits rise.
This is hardly a major windfall, because the government must also pay more for its own operations, including military activities, government transportation, heating public buildings, flights and other energy-intensive services.
4. Price-Control Measures Begin to Lose Their Effectiveness
When the war began and the Strait of Hormuz was disrupted, some observers argued that the strategy had failed because oil prices had not reached $150, $200 or even $250 a barrel.
One reason prices initially remained below those levels was the existence of enormous oil inventories worldwide, particularly in China, whose reserves are unmatched by those of other countries.
But as the conflict has dragged on, with the Strait of Hormuz entering its eighth month of disruption, restrictions affecting Saudi Arabia's access to the Red Sea and attacks deep inside Saudi territory have transformed the energy crisis. The mechanisms that had helped contain prices are now being depleted.
Under an agreement reached in March amid the war, members of the International Energy Agency, which includes major oil-consuming countries, agreed to release a combined 400 million barrels from strategic reserves worldwide. The U.S. share of that release was set at 172 million barrels.
The increasingly difficult situation has nevertheless prompted the United States in recent days to resort to an emergency release of around 400,000 barrels of oil.
The contrast between the original reserve-release agreement and the much smaller emergency delivery illustrates the growing pressure on the United States as the disruption continues.
Flames of War Against Iran Spread to the Streets of Europe
WANA (Oct 24) &#8211; As the war against Iran continues and disruptions to global energy markets persist, a wave of protests driven by economic and livelihood concerns has emerged across six European countries. Rising living costs and declining purchasing power have added to the scale of the unrest. Protests across the six countries first [&hellip;]
The United Arab Emirates played a central role in efforts to circumvent restrictions on oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz. According to this account, the UAE covertly moved small oil shipments out of the strait through the southern corridor using smaller vessels, transferring the cargo to supertankers outside the restricted area.
Iran and the Axis of Resistance developed three measures to counter these activities, all of which are now being implemented.
The first involved targeting smaller vessels engaged in oil smuggling. Initially, damage to a few ships had limited impact because the vessels and their cargoes were relatively inexpensive, while the operation had government backing. Consequently, attacks intensified to the point where a larger proportion of the vessels could be targeted.
The second measure involved attacks on supertankers across a wider geographical area than the previously designated restricted zone.
Given the high value of these vessels and their cargoes, such attacks could have a substantial financial impact. A supertanker capable of carrying two million barrels of oil can be worth between $150 million and $200 million. At an average oil price of $100 per barrel, its cargo would be worth another $200 million.
Although some attacks merely disable tankers, more destructive strikes that result in their complete destruction can wipe out hundreds of millions of dollars in combined vessel and cargo value.
WANA (Oct 23) &#8211; The renewed rise in oil prices has once again drawn attention to the infrastructure that supplies the energy needed by the global economy. Facilities in Saudi Arabia, the United Arab Emirates, Qatar, Kuwait and Bahrain could have consequences extending far beyond the region if disrupted. When International Energy Agency Executive [&hellip;]
The third major measure involved an escalation of Yemeni attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure. These operations included strikes on oil tankers in the Red Sea, oil terminals in Yanbu, oil pipelines, refineries and even administrative buildings housing senior executives and employees of Saudi Aramco.
In short, the expansion of attacks on smaller oil-smuggling vessels, the widening of the geographical area in which supertankers are targeted, and broader attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure have placed considerable upward pressure on oil prices and refined petroleum products.
As a result, even without a full-scale war across the region, energy prices have begun to resemble levels typically associated with wartime conditions.
Meanwhile, the United States has continued intensive efforts to draw on the support of France, Russia and India to address the diesel shortage. Trump, Steve Witkoff, Jared Kushner and Marco Rubio have all been involved in these efforts, but they have yet to produce a significant result.

### 51. ★★★☆☆ Ukraine’s military says Starlink satellite connection stable after recent restrictions - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T15:31:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukraine’s military says Starlink satellite connection stable after recent restrictions&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, previously warned any restriction to Starshield system would ‘bring a lot of human losses’. Photograph: Sergey Dolzhenko/EPA
Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, previously warned any restriction to Starshield system would ‘bring a lot of human losses’. Photograph: Sergey Dolzhenko/EPA
Ukraine’s military says Starlink satellite connection stable after recent restrictions
But Elon Musk, whose company SpaceX owns Starlink, accuses Ukrainian military of violating ⁠the company’s terms
Ukraine’s military has a “stable connection to Starlink”, the Ukrainian government said, 10 days after its ability to use the satellite communications system was suddenly restricted .
But hours after the announcement, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk, whose company owns Starlink, accused the Ukrainian military of violating ⁠the company’s terms. Neither Musk nor the US government, which operates Starshield using Starlink technology, took responsibility for imposing the curbs.
“Ukraine military violated Starlink terms of service and used it for ⁠offensive drone strikes to kill people. That loophole was closed last week,” Musk said in a social media post .
Serhiy Beskrestnov, a Ukrainian presidential adviser, said on the Telegram app that the country is “physically unable” to use Starlink for long- and medium-range attack drone operations.
Ukraine’s general staff and defence ministry did not immediately comment.
The Starlink satellite terminals, which are produced by Elon Musk’s SpaceX but managed by the US government, are a key component of Ukraine’s military operations. Sudden speed restrictions on the terminals, which capped operations at speeds of 160km/h (99mph), led to the failure of several Ukrainian combat missions earlier this month.
The military’s ability to intercept Russian attack drones was hampered and some Ukrainian operations to strike behind enemy frontlines were cancelled. Russia was able to launch ballistic missiles at Ukraine without any advance warning from the US.
The speed restrictions coincided with the announcement by Donald Trump of a US deal to buy Russian diesel, although the US president initially denied that his administration was behind the decision to cut intelligence sharing with Ukraine.
“I don’t know if it was anybody, but it wasn’t me, and I don’t think it was Elon,” Trump said, referring to Musk, SpaceX’s chief executive.
However, the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, appeared to throw suspicion on Musk when he defended Trump last Wednesday. “The president has nothing to do with the decisions that are made by Starlink and Starshield and by Mr Musk,” Rubio said. “That’s a private company. The president doesn’t run that company and can’t direct that company to do anything.”
Musk was quick to blame the limits on Starshield on the Trump administration , writing on X last week that the systems were “owned and controlled by the US government”. “Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately Potus [president of the United States],” he wrote.
Musk blamed the limits on Starshield on the Trump administration, writing on X that the systems were ‘owned and controlled by the US government’. Photograph: Evan Vucci/Reuters The US vice-president, JD Vance, backed Musk on Thursday, saying the billionaire “doesn’t want to get in the middle of what is a war between two state actors without the United States government effectively directing him on what to do”.
Ukraine’s president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy , blamed both parties: “It was a message from [the] American government to limit the speed … And technically, SpaceX did it.”
There were no similar restrictions on Starshield terminals in EU countries and that they applied only to Ukraine, he added. “In other words, this is a planned operation.”
He said the decision would “bring a lot of human losses … of course people would die”.
Trump, Musk, Zelenskyy: who is blaming whom in the Ukraine-Starlink row?
Announcing the restoration of the system on Saturday in a diplomatic video address on the Telegram app, Zelenskyy expressed his gratitude to his US allies, including Trump and the US state department, for resolving the operational issue: “The most important development for ‌today is that Starlink ‌and Starshield have resumed operations at their pre-restriction speeds. The speeds are now at the ‌level we need.”
The restoration began overnight. “By this morning, the speeds were already sufficient for operational use,” Zelenskyy said. “This is very important and significant for our frontline logistics and for defending Ukraine against jet-powered Shaheds.”
On Thursday, Trump had claimed the service was running again at full speed, but a European official speaking to the Associated Press denied the service was fully functioning.
When asked by reporters whether he would tell Musk to stop “messing around” with Ukraine’s access to Starlink on Friday, the US president said: “I’ve not spoken to him. I might have to … I’ve heard it was fixed. But if it’s not, I’ll speak to him … If there is a problem, I’ll get it straightened out.”
He then described Musk as a “very brilliant” and “very good” person.

### 52. ★★★☆☆ Can Amazon Take Market Share From Nvidia in the AI Chip Race? - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T16:15:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Can Amazon Take Market Share From Nvidia in the AI Chip Race?&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Amazon ( AMZN -0.95% ) recently launched a new software tool that will allow its custom Trainium AI chips to run code written in the most popular AI coding language without modification. It&#x27;s a move aimed at boosting the potential for Amazon&#x27;s custom application-specific integrated circuits (ASICs) to take market share away from Nvidia &#x27;s ( NVDA -0.59% ) widely used graphics processing units as demand for AI chips continues to swell.
Amazon isn&#x27;t the only tech giant working on its own AI chips for commercial use. Alphabet  recently began making its custom AI chips available for sale directly to enterprises, rather than limiting them to being leased through Google Cloud. However, investors shouldn&#x27;t expect these developments to have a meaningful effect on Nvidia&#x27;s financial results.
Amazon&#x27;s AI chip business wrapped up the second quarter with a $25 billion annual revenue run rate. That&#x27;s not small, but it pales in comparison to the $96.2 billion in revenue Nvidia booked in its fiscal 2027 second quarter alone, or the more than $300 billion it booked over the past four quarters.
Market cap Market cap calculated using publicly traded shares outstanding only. Does not include unlisted, private, or dual-class non-traded shares. Implied market cap may vary. $2.68T
While Amazon&#x27;s push to commercialize its custom chips could boost sales, the largest tech companies are still rushing to buy Nvidia chips. That&#x27;s how Nvidia managed to produce 106% year-over-year revenue growth in its most recent quarter, and CEO Jensen Huang says that AI demand is still accelerating.
If anything, Amazon&#x27;s AI chips can become niche hardware in the industry. Since the AI build-out has been so expansive, it may provide enough room for high, sustained revenue growth rates. If Amazon&#x27;s chip business can generate net profit margins similar to Nvidia&#x27;s, that should be enough to boost Amazon&#x27;s stock price.
Another point supporting the view that Amazon&#x27;s AI chips won&#x27;t significantly impact Nvidia&#x27;s business is that Amazon is still buying Nvidia chips. In fact, it has built up a stockpile of billions of dollars&#x27; worth of them that it doesn&#x27;t yet have the data center infrastructure and connected electricity supply to power up.
Nvidia&#x27;s processors have become the most reliable option for providing high-end compute in an industry that values speed and will pay a premium for flawless execution. Even as Amazon touts the value of its own chips for the narrower workloads they are designed to handle, for flexible compute power, the tech leader still turns to Nvidia.
Notably, those stockpiled supplies of chips and data center servers highlight that electrical supplies will remain a major bottleneck for AI infrastructure expansion. Still, hyperscalers aren&#x27;t waiting for that problem to be resolved completely, and continue to accelerate their capital expenditures.
Nvidia&#x27;s guidance implies that its business is still growing at a substantial rate despite the intensifying competition. Its fiscal 2027 third-quarter guidance calls for $108 billion in revenue at the midpoint, which would amount to 12.3% sequential growth. That guidance supports Huang&#x27;s claim that AI demand is accelerating.
While more rivals are likely to enter the expanding AI chip market, Nvidia remains the clear leader in data center chips.

### 54. ★★☆☆☆ The bitter row over military service that could shape Israel's future

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **假设性事件**: 是（已降权）
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T23:03:21+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The ultra-Orthodox community is resisting being drafted into the Israeli army. But will they now bring down the Netanyahu government?

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Furious protesters are denouncing Israeli security forces as "Nazis" - but this is not a scene from a pro-Palestinian rally on the streets of London or Paris. This clash is in the heart of Israel, in which hundreds of ultra-Orthodox men in white shirts, black fedoras and long black frock coats face off against the military police.
The flashpoint is a building in the city of Modi&#x27;in, midway between Jerusalem and Tel Aviv, where a young ultra-Orthodox Jewish (or Haredi) man lives. The police have come to arrest him after he failed to report for military service.
The clash in early October, seen by the BBC in a video, is one of many in recent months in which one of the deepest schisms in Israeli society has become ever more exposed.
This widening fault line is playing out against the backdrop of the wars Israel is currently fighting, including the unresolved conflict against Hamas in Gaza - as well as increasing violence by extremist settlers against Palestinians in the occupied West Bank.
Once a tiny minority, the ultra-Orthodox remain a group apart even as their numbers expand year by year. Currently, they are estimated to make up about 14% of the population, and the birth rate within the community remains far higher than among other Israelis. If their numbers continue to grow at a similar rate, they could make up one-third of the Jewish Israeli population by 2050.
As the ultra-Orthodox community becomes bigger, so has the debate around the expense of supporting them with stipends so they can devote themselves to full-time religious study. Subsidies to the group are estimated to cost the government 35bn shekels (Â£8.4bn) per year and, until recently, they were exempted from military service.
Now they&#x27;re no longer an insignificant minority, they&#x27;re facing pressure to engage more with wider Israeli society.
Image caption, Ultra-Orthodox Jews have staged many protests against being drafted into the military
National security and the economy are two of the key issues this election. That means the division between the ultra-Orthodox and the majority of Israelis who believe they are not contributing their fair share to either is starker than ever.
With no outright majority likely for any current political alliance, and the future of Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in doubt, the role of smaller parties - including Haredi parties - will once again be crucial in deciding who will govern Israel.
To have any chance of staying in power, Netanyahu needs to keep the ultra-Orthodox parties on side, however much pressure there is within Israel for their special status to be diminished.
From the ultra-Orthodox perspective, their social contract with the Israeli state is increasingly coming under pressure. Their all but exclusive focus on the study of Judaism&#x27;s religious texts - such as the Torah, the Jewish Bible - means their education system is separate and many Haredim are not part of Israel&#x27;s workforce.
For the ultra-Orthodox, the biggest issue of all is holding on to the exemption from serving in the IDF for students at traditional religious - or yeshiva - institutions. This dates back to the earliest days of the Israeli state when the Haredim were a miniscule community of survivors struggling to rebuild from the near extinction of the Holocaust.
But as Israel has become more embattled after the attacks of October 7, fighting wars on several fronts, their near complete absence from the country&#x27;s conscription army has become more controversial.
Religious students and their rabbis believe that if young ultra-Orthodox men enter the military, they will be exposed to influences and behaviours that may decisively turn them away from their community. Beyond that, they believe that their staying true to the traditions of their community is vital for the very survival not only of Israel but the Jewish people.
When asked not long after October 7 why the ultra-Orthodox didn&#x27;t contribute more to the military in such an emergency, one of the community&#x27;s most senior leaders, Rabbi Dov Landau, said that not only should religious students not serve in the army, but they shouldn&#x27;t even visit the wounded or attend the funerals of soldiers.
Image caption, The Haredi birth rate is higher than in the rest of Israeli society
The rabbi said that in a time of such danger, only Torah students could protect Israel spiritually. "Salvation only comes through Torah learning, orderly Torah learning. And any neglect of or disruption to Torah learning, woe and alas for what will happenâ¦ The more Torah learners multiply, the more Torah learners increase their learning, the better. They protect us."
It would be a considerable understatement to say that this view is not shared by wider Israeli society. Polls have shown public support for conscripting the ultra-Orthodox surge throughout the war in Gaza. Many see it as a dereliction of responsibility by the Haredi community, made all the more galling by the fact that such a stance comes at the expense of taxpayers. The IDF has bemoaned a deficit in manpower, while many Israelis have had to serve hundreds of days in the past three years.
Dahlia Scheindlin is a leading political consultant and pollster in Israel. She says this issue is critical for Netanyahu and is key to understanding this moment in Israeli society against the backdrop of the war in Gaza, particularly among the political right. "Really the most burning, more immediate issue is the right-wing communities who are furious at the government for protecting the blanket exemption from the IDF at a time when, especially among the right, but really everybody in the Jewish community in Israel, is serving and many dying in these wars."
Two years ago, the Supreme Court ruled that the exemption for ultra-Orthodox men from serving in the military was effectively null and void. That could have meant that those dedicated to full-time religious study would now be drafted.
The reaction from the Haredi community was one of fury. Hundreds of thousands of Haredim held protests, blocking the main highways. And as with the incident in Modi&#x27;in, they tried to prevent the arrest of religious students for evading conscription. Protests have continued off and on, and densely-populated Haredi areas are often the backdrops for confrontation with the security forces.
There are two parties that have traditionally represented the Haredi community. One is United Torah Judaism â or UTJ â and represents Ashkenazi Jews, or Jews of European origin. The other is Shas, which was formed later and represents the Sephardi and Mizrahi â Jews from North Africa and the Middle East. They share a core agenda, which puts religious study above all else, including any questions around relations with the Palestinians.
Both UTJ and Shas have been part of Netanyahu&#x27;s coalition - and they used their political clout to find ways to try to block enforcement of the Supreme Court ruling. On several occasions, they threatened to pull out of the alliance and bring the government down. UTJ acted on this but continued to support the government from outside, while Shas remained within the coalition.
Centre-right politician Gadi Eisenkot, who&#x27;s shaped up as Netanyahu&#x27;s main rival in this month&#x27;s election, does not support their stance. He has made clear his belief that the idea of the ultra-Orthodox constituting a separate state within Israel with its own rules has become increasingly toxic. "A country that sends its best sons and daughters to fight and sacrifice their lives cannot tolerate a reality in which a state within a state exists," he said.
All this has meant that the issue of conscription exemption for ultra-Orthodox men is the number one priority on the electoral agenda of both Shas and UTJ.
A recent remark by a UTJ parliamentary candidate, Eliakim Stark, has raised the temperature further. He compared the plight of ultra-Orthodox men evading the military draft to the persecution of Jews during the Holocaust. It triggered furious rebukes from opposition leaders from across the political spectrum.
Daniel Goldman, an activist who specialises in Haredi issues, says that the two main Haredi parties feel increasingly embattled and less open to compromise.
Image caption, Protesters have blocked key highways
He says the ultra-Orthodox don&#x27;t believe that the state really needs their boys to bolster numbers. "It&#x27;s at least partly down to the fact that the Haredi parties see the state as wanting to take their boys out of the community and change them from ultra-Orthodox to either regular religious or secular, God forbid, as they perceive it," he says. "And so in that environment of tension, they&#x27;ve actually hardened their positions."
Yitzhak Goldknopf from the UTJ, a former minister, has gone so far as to issue an ultimatum: "The Haredim will not change their way of life because there is an army and a war... If there is no choice and they must leave the country, they will leave."
But this is having a clear impact on the electoral fortunes of the Netanyahu coalition - of which Shas in particular has long been a key part. Without them, Netanyahu&#x27;s alliance would fall short of the more than 60 seats needed for a majority in the Knesset.
Dahlia Scheindlin, the Israeli pollster, says that the refusal to give up the draft exemption by Shas and UTJ is damaging Netanyahu&#x27;s coalition. "It is one of the main reasons why the coalition is unable to win back the kind of support that they had in 2022 when they won 64 seats," she says. "They&#x27;re currently polling at about 50 to 52 seats."
All Netanyahu&#x27;s challengers advocate for ending the ultra-Orthodox exemption and cutting funds to institutions or individuals who refuse the draft. On the left, Yair Golan has called for an immediate mobilisation of those who are eligible.
Image caption, Benjamin Netanyahu is relying on Haredi support which may not materialise
So, despite statements from Shas and UTJ that they would switch allegiance from Netanyahu if he were to dilute his support, there seems for now nowhere else for them to go.
Anshel Pfeffer has been a leading commentator on the Israeli political scene for years - now mainly as the Economist&#x27;s correspondent in Israel. He says Netanyahu&#x27;s position looks shaky. "There are two things that are bringing together the current opposition bloc," he says. "One is obviously they want to replace Netanyahu and the other is they are capitalising on this really hostile environment nowadays towards the ultra-Orthodox, mainly over the issue of the draft to the army.
"Netanyahu has been willing for many years, to basically give the ultra-Orthodox whatever they want. They&#x27;re not going to get that from the opposition."
Into this arena of resentment and intransigence, a new Haredi party has emerged this year, presenting itself as a break with the traditional mindset of Shas and UTJ.
Essentially, the Haredi Public Party is offering a compromise in which the ultra-Orthodox would contribute more to Israel as a whole while maintaining the core of their identity.
For the first time in the ultra-Orthodox community, it is also fielding two female candidates.
One of them, Ziva Ginz, explained to the BBC her party&#x27;s position on the military draft. Key to it is men serving being allowed to remain culturally Haredi. "Anyone whose life is genuinely devoted to full-time Torah study should be able to continue learning. Everyone who isn&#x27;t sitting and studying for a full day should serve alongside the rest of Israeli youth, in frameworks where a young man can enter as a Haredi and finish as a Haredi," she says.
But it may be too soon for such efforts at compromise to make an impact this election.
Anshel Pfeffer says that it&#x27;s difficult to sway the base vote in the ultra-Orthodox community, where most still essentially follow what their rabbi says they should do.
"And once people are voting for other parties, they won&#x27;t necessarily do so for another Haredi party," Pfeffer explains. "That&#x27;s why we&#x27;re seeing people like [far-right leader and National Security Minister Itamar] Ben-Gvir making inroads in the Haredi community, because he&#x27;s the only non-Haredi party leader who actually talks to young Haredi. Basically, every other party in Israel doesn&#x27;t even try."
Image caption, Ben-Gvir is making inroads with Haredi voters
This is reflected in the polls, which show his Jewish Power party is making gains.
If Ben Gvir&#x27;s actions are increasingly seen as tainting Israel&#x27;s international image with his support for policies such as annexing the occupied West Bank, his hard-right message is attracting a bigger core of support within the country. His ultra-nationalist ethos is not one that traditionally overlaps with policies of the Haredi parties, but they share a position on the right of Israeli politics.
So for some ultra-Orthodox voters looking for a new political home, Ben Gvir represents the nearest alternative. His self-portrayal as a strong man unafraid to take an extreme position against those he perceives as Israel&#x27;s enemies in the face of international condemnation may attract some already leaning to the far right in the Haredi community.
Image caption, Gadi Eisenkot has been firm in his support for Haredi Jews serving in the military
No alliance appears close to achieving a majority, however, so there is bound to be a lot of horse trading to secure a workable coalition after the vote.
Netanyahu will naturally apply his exceptional political skills to trying to find one way or another to stay in power.
As ever, that leaves the smaller parties with the ability to play the role of kingmaker. That&#x27;s a role that the ultra-Orthodox parties have been known for in the past. But this time they may not be the decisive factor.
"The truth is, any small party can become a kingmaker," Dahlia Scheindlin says. "I don&#x27;t think it would take too much to lure the Haredi parties away from Netanyahu... The thing is, though, I don&#x27;t think the opposition parties will want to lure them in unless they have some promise that they&#x27;re really prepared to make serious concessions on the draft. The opposition parties are absolutely staking their platforms and their promise to the voters on that issue."
This time, rather than being the kingmakers, it may turn out that the ultra-Orthodox parties help bring down the king.
Spain and China are cosying up - to the anger of many in the EU
The Republican candidates walking a Trump tightrope
China has cracked down on AI relationships. Is it ahead of the game?

### 56. ★★☆☆☆ Why is Egypt so wary of getting involved in Yemen’s conflict?

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T07:59:38+00:00
- **发生了什么**: As Egypt considers defending Saudi Arabia against Houthi attacks, memories of its war in Yemen in the 1960s loom large.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Why is Egypt so wary of getting involved in Yemen’s conflict? on social media
Three Egyptian soldiers who surrendered during the fighting in Yemen on April 15, 1963 [Jim Pringle/AP Photo]
Egyptian forces arrived in Yemen in the thousands, backing the country’s government in a civil war against rebels that threatened to overthrow it.
But this is not today’s war – this is 1962, and the Egyptians were supporting republican forces in North Yemen against the country’s royalists. The intervention came after Yemenis who were friendlier to the Arab nationalist ideology propagated by Egypt under President Gamal Abdel Nasser led a revolution that overthrew Zaidi Imam Mohammad al-Badr – a theocratic monarch.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 US-Iran war, then and now: How the landscape has changed since February
list 2 of 3 Saudi Arabia security puts Asian Cup in doubt: Japan football chief
list 3 of 3 Are global oil reserves sufficient to withstand renewed US-Iran war?
Although the republican side was ultimately successful in the war against the Saudi-backed royalists, Egyptian forces withdrew in the wake of their defeat in the 1967 war against Israel , and after also suffering heavy losses in Yemen.
Suggestions of a new military intervention by Egypt in Yemen today, after comments by President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi, are therefore bringing back memories of that period for Egyptians.
Following some backlash against the suggestion of direct military intervention, el-Sisi emphasised on Wednesday that any military deployment would be focused on defending Saudi Arabia, and would not involve any direct aggression against Yemen’s Houthi rebels.
And on Thursday, Egyptian Foreign Minister Badr Abdelatty called for a political solution to the conflict.
Saudi King Faisal and Egyptian President Nasser shake hands in Khartoum, Sudan, in the presence of Sudanese Prime Minister Muhammad Ahmad Mahgoub, in the centre, August 31, 1967, after an earlier meeting in which the two agreed to a formula to end their participation in the Yemen civil war on opposing sides [AP Photo] What has the Egyptian government said it will do?
After Saudi Arabia came under more intense attacks from the Houthis, including a strike on King Khalid  international airport  in Riyadh which killed 13 people, including one Egyptian, earlier this month, it appears that the kingdom expects support from its longtime allies, including Egypt.
Some countries, including Turkiye, Pakistan, France and the United Kingdom , have already deployed forces to Saudi Arabia in defensive operations or have indicated they are willing to do so.
Saudi Arabia has faced repeated Houthi ballistic missile and drone attacks as the war in Yemen escalated over the past few months, particularly after a Saudi-backed Yemeni government counteroffensive against the Houthis began in early October. Saudi Arabia has supported a counteroffensive by forces allied to the internationally recognised government, with air raids against the Houthis.
Egypt’s National Defence Council said on October 18 the country was ready to provide “all necessary and possible support to Saudi Arabia to defend its safety and stability”.
While much has been made of the possibility of an Egyptian deployment, Cairo has repeatedly made it clear that it has no intention of involving itself in direct attacks against the Houthis.
But even a defensive combat mission would involve the deployment of Egyptian forces outside the country’s borders, so it requires approval from the Egyptian parliament. A vote is scheduled for Monday, after a weeklong delay to allow reticent lawmakers to deliberate on this move.
Saudi-backed Yemeni government fighters ride on the back of a truck during battles against Yemen’s Iran-backed Houthis at the Jabal Han front west of the city of Taiz on October 21, 2026 [AFP] Why is Egypt wary of becoming involved in Yemen?
This apprehension over military involvement in Yemen is largely related to the Egyptian government’s desire to avoid foreign military entanglements and direct involvement in the US-Israel war on Iran and its allies.
Active participation in attacks against the Houthis could open Egypt up to retaliation by the group or Iran. The Houthis have already shown they are capable of pinpoint attacks on vital civilian and military targets in Saudi Arabia.
An expansion of Houthi attacks on Red Sea shipping – both Egyptian and otherwise – would also be highly damaging to Egypt’s economy, as the country controls its northern entry point, the Suez Canal, and is heavily reliant on this waterway for trade.
Fewer ships passing through the Red Sea means fewer fees paid to the Egyptian government for their passage through the Suez Canal. El-Sisi has said Egypt has already lost $20bn in potential revenues over the last few years due to regional instability, including previous attacks on ships in the Red Sea and the turmoil in the Strait of Hormuz caused by the US-Israel war on Iran.
Disruptions to energy supplies via the Bab al-Mandeb now, which the Houthis effectively control, would likely lead to higher oil prices and knock-on effects for net consumers such as Egypt.
There is also the historical aspect. While Egypt can argue that its intervention in Yemen in the 1960s was an overall success, with the Republican side eventually emerging victorious, the campaign in mountainous northwestern Yemen was brutal. Thousands of Egyptian soldiers were killed in the campaign, and this was blamed by some for the Egyptian military’s lack of readiness in confronting Israel in the 1967 war.
“There is the memory that [Egypt] went to Yemen and faced forces supported by Saudi Arabia, and Egyptian troops were killed there,” said Ahmad Dahshan, the editor-in-chief of the Center of Arabic Eurasian Studies. “Then [the mentality is] that Egypt will send troops to Yemen once again, where they would also be killed, but this time on behalf of Saudi Arabia.”
Egyptian soldiers march during a parade marking Cyprus’s Independence Day in Nicosia, October 1, 2026 [Yiannis Kourtoglou/Reuters] How could Egypt support Saudi Arabia?
While Egypt has not detailed what “defensive” military assistance it could provide, judging by the support supplied by other countries aiding Saudi Arabia, this could include operating air defence equipment and interceptors to protect the kingdom from missiles and drones or guarding sensitive sites.
Even this could lead to the deaths of foreign soldiers, as Houthi strikes have so far targeted sites in Saudi Arabia they perceive to be strategically important, including those that Egyptian personnel could be deployed to.
There is also potential for naval assistance in the Red Sea to protect shipping, and forces stationed on the Saudi-Yemeni border to reinforce security there, which have also been targets.
“These troops would only be deployed, if they are deployed at all, to contribute to the protection of Saudi territory,” Dahshan said. “The discussion this time may be taking place because there is a possibility of ground troops being deployed to protect the Saudi border against any attempted Houthi incursion.”
Egypt was officially part of the Saudi-led coalition to restore the Yemeni government in 2015, but played only a limited role in operations. However, Saudi Arabia and the United Arab Emirates have played decisive roles during the war, with other countries such as Sudan at times contributing fighters on the ground.
Saudi Arabia and the UAE not only conducted heavy bombing campaigns against the Houthis, but also had forces in the country coordinating with the Yemeni military and anti-Houthi groups. This exposed both countries to Houthi attacks.
This young Arab dances in a public square in Sanaa, Yemen, on September 26, 1966, while the band plays to celebrate the revolution on September 26, 1962, which ended 11 centuries of monarchy in Yemen [AP Photo] But some Yemenis on the anti-Houthi side blame foreign forces, in particular the UAE, for undermining the independence of the Yemeni government. The UAE was allegedly a major supporter of the Southern Transitional Council, and was eventually forced out of Yemen at the start of the year following a Saudi-backed Yemeni government push to defeat southern secessionists.
While Saudi air support has been a huge advantage for Yemeni government forces over the Houthis, many observers say that the pro-government side does not need additional manpower on the ground.
“The Yemeni government just needs political support and economic support, and that has been the case since the war started,” said Mohammed al-Qadhi, an independent Yemeni political and conflict analyst. “The Yemeni government has enough fighters … it lacks weapons and economic support, as well as logistical and technical support. It needs better coordination and command and control for the forces on the ground.”
As for the prospect of any Egyptian involvement, al-Qadhi said many Yemenis remain indebted to Egypt for the support it has provided the republic in the past.
“Yemenis still cherish the Egyptian role with pride and respect … Without the Egyptian role and Nasser’s support, the republic would not have existed,” al-Qadhi said. “The Houthis recall history and how the Egyptian support in defending the republic was vital … though I dismiss a similar role for the Egyptian army today.”

</details>
