### 57. ★★☆☆☆ Bots, deepfakes and surveillance: Israel’s election goes to war online

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-25T05:27:54+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Netanyahu and his rivals are tied days from the vote, and both camps are fighting with bots, deepfakes and data.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Bots, deepfakes and surveillance: Israel’s election goes to war online on social media
A print house worker produces a Likud party election campaign poster showing Israeli prime minister and Likud party leader, Benjamin Netanyahu [Amir Levy/Getty Images]
Polls show the blocs linked to Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu and the opposition, led by former Chief of Staff Gadi Eisenkot, are locked in a virtual tie before Tuesday’s general election.
Efforts to mobilise supporters, win voters and attack opponents are being played out online through a series of social media advertisements and doctored AI videos. Many of these paint a bleak vision, the outcome depending on whether the party is on the right or centre, of Israel if their rivals win.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 The kingmakers who could determine Israel’s election
list 2 of 4 In Israel election, Palestinian citizens weigh fear against disillusionment
list 3 of 4 Israel preparing full lockdown of occupied West Bank after Hebron gunfight
list 4 of 4 Ultra-Orthodox conscription debate ignites Israel’s election battleground
On Thursday, the Israeli liberal daily Haaretz reported that dozens of online bots created to undermine the Iranian government were being repurposed to spread campaign propaganda supporting Netanyahu and his Likud party, and warn voters of the dangers posed by Eisenkot and former Prime Minister Naftali Bennett, who make up part of the opposition bloc .
Both camps are pushing AI-generated and deepfake videos on X. Several of them show Netanyahu confronting his alleged failings before the Hamas-led attack of October 7, 2023; others show the opposition being sympathetic to Arab parties (those representing the Palestinian citizens of Israel) which have been perceived by many as kingmakers in the coming election.
“We’re competing against parties with far greater budgets and technological resources, particularly the Likud,” Mauricio Lapchik, director of the Arab-dominated Joint List Hebrew-language campaign, said. “The use of technology is clearly deepening an already unequal playing field … We may not have the same resources, but we are convinced that our message matters more than the artificial technology behind it.”
Significant resources have been used by the Israeli right against the left-wing Joint List – an alliance of Hadash, Balad and Ta’al – and Ra’am, also known as the United Arab List. Palestinian citizens of Israel generally support one of these two blocs, and all the parties have faced legal challenges before the parliamentary election .
Likud were looking to use Elector, an app that tracks voter turnout on election day, but were restricted by the Central Elections Committee due to privacy concerns. They will instead use a new app named Victory 26 . Unlike Elector, this does not reveal the identities of voters but would allow Likud activists and officials to determine which areas have the lowest turnout, manually take the names of people who have cast their votes, and then target those who have not yet.
Likud is encouraging supporters and potential sympathisers to download and use the app on election day, according to Haaretz.
This app could play a crucial role in the election, which will likely be determined by voter turnout.
“Elector and similar programmes are very basic compared to what the Israeli military is capable of now,” Sebastian Ben Daniel, a doctoral researcher into algorithms, computability, and AI, said. “Israel has the perfect laboratory with which to develop this kind of technology.”
An Israeli soldier stands under a surveillance camera at a checkpoint in the flashpoint Palestinian city of Hebron. Israel’s army has deployed a sweeping personal data collection programme using facial recognition technology against Palestinians in parts of the occupied West Bank [File: Hazem Bader/AFP] Slight gains/fierce fight
In recent weeks, Finance Minister Belazel Smotrich caused uproar when he released an AI video showing the Supreme Court’s president, a justice and the attorney general – all frequent targets of the far-right government who have sought to shake off judicial oversight – being violently dragged from an antigovernment protest.
In another AI video shared by Netanyahu earlier this year, his key political rival Eisenkot is seen running towards an AI figure who many believe resembled his son, Gal, who was killed while taking part in the genocide on Gaza, before passing him entirely and embracing the head of the Arab Ra’am party, Mansour Abbas.
Netanyahu’s camp later said they did not intend the figure to mirror Eisenkot’s son, who wears a T-shirt saying “Israel’s Unity” in Hebrew, and replaced the male character with a female one.
There has also been heavy use of AI to demonise Palestinian citizens of Israel, with a video shared by far-right National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir portraying the people of Gaza as donkeys, and “herding” them to Paris. Ben-Gvir has called for the ethnic cleansing of Gaza via “voluntary emigration” and frequently targets Palestinians in his political stunts, such as humiliating detainees and prisoners in Israeli jails .
There has also been a heavy use of bots by Netanyahu and Ben-Gvir’s campaign to amplify their political messaging, according to numerous accounts.
“The online campaign has been especially violent. AI, algorithms and data management are all utilised to cement what we call ‘affective polarisation’,” Roi Zur a lecturer at Essex University and specialist in voting behaviour and election strategies, told Al Jazeera. This term is used by political scientists to refer to the entrenchment of political blocs and an increased animosity towards the opposing side.
“Affective polarisation targets people at the fringe, who might be thinking of voting for a party in an opposing bloc, and bring them back into the fold.” As this election will likely be determined by the success of the smaller parties and voter turnout in general, this is a pertinent point.
“Turnout and management of the parties will be essential. If you look at large secular cities, such as Tel Aviv or Haifa, turnout is typically only 60 percent,” he said. “If you compare that to cities and towns with a large Haredi (ultra -Orthodox) population, such as Jerusalem and Bnei Brak, the turnout is around 80 to 85 percent, so a large part of the campaigning is also to get people out to vote by threatening them with the ‘other’.”

</details>

### 59. ★★☆☆☆ Kim Jong-un crosses newly opened bridge into China

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-24T13:34:56+00:00

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Wang Huning (front right), a member of the standing committee of China’s Communist party accompanied Kim Jong-un (front left) to the bridge. Photograph: Xinhua/Shutterstock
Wang Huning (front right), a member of the standing committee of China’s Communist party accompanied Kim Jong-un (front left) to the bridge. Photograph: Xinhua/Shutterstock
North Korean leader travels over New Yalu River Bridge to meet senior Communist party member
Kim Jong-un has visited China, inaugurating a bridge that connects the two countries and saying he hopes to expand cooperation with his neighbour.
The North Korean leader arrived in the Chinese border city of Dandong on Saturday after crossing the New Yalu River Bridge, according to South Korea’s unification ministry, which handles affairs related to North Korea, and China’s official Xinhua news agency.
He met Wang Huning, a member of the standing committee of China’s Communist party. The pair toured a car factory, and promised to strengthen the bilateral relationship.
Kim said his country “stands ready to work with China to carry forward their historical friendship and expand practical cooperation, thereby delivering tangible benefits to the peoples of both countries”, according to Xinhua.
Wang then accompanied Kim to the bridge to send him off, Xinhua said.
North Korea has opened up bridges to Russia and China – in which direction will it go?
The visit appears to be timed to underscore the strength of Kim’s ties with Beijing ahead of the 25 October anniversary of China’s first major victory in the Korean war, which came days after Chinese troops crossed the Yalu River into North Korea in October 1950.
Japan’s Kyodo news agency said on Saturday that traffic controls had been imposed around the bridge and near a museum about China’s role in the war, and that hotel bookings nearby had been restricted.
Diplomatic sources told the agency the measures were linked to a visit by a senior North Korean official.
The 3km bridge opened in mid-October , 12 years after China finished building it, after being held up by delays on the North Korean side. In early September, North Korea and Russia opened the first road bridge across their own short border, leaving Pyongyang newly linked by road to its main backers.
The trip is Kim’s sixth visit to China and his first since September 2025, when he attended a military parade in Beijing alongside the Chinese president, Xi Jinping, and Vladimir Putin. Xi visited Pyongyang in June , his first trip there in seven years.
Relations with South Korea, meanwhile, have deteriorated. Kim’s sister, Kim Yo Jong, has issued about 10 statements criticising Seoul since late September, a frequency South Korea’s unification ministry has called unusual, and has also announced plans to resume loudspeaker broadcasts at the border within days.
Xi will host the Asia Pacific Economic Cooperation summit in Shenzhen on 18-19 November, which has fuelled speculation about a meeting between Kim and Donald Trump.
Reuters reported on Friday , however, that US and South Korean officials see little chance of Kim meeting the US president this year.
North Korea has opened up bridges to Russia and China – in which direction will it go?

### 60. ★★☆☆☆ Andy Burnham says he wants ‘honest discussion’ about freedom of movement with EU

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲

- **发生了什么**: Prime minister is considering different options for new relationship with Brussels ahead of summit in NovemberAndy Burnham says he wants an “honest discussion” with the British public about the possibility of a return to free movement between the UK and the EU as part of his plans for a new relationship with the bloc.The prime minister has been open about his desire to revisit the relationship between Britain and Brussels. He has said a new deal could mean the <a href="https://w

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Andy Burnham said he believed the ‘balance of migration’ had changed in recent years. Photograph: Temilade Adelaja/Reuters
Andy Burnham said he believed the ‘balance of migration’ had changed in recent years. Photograph: Temilade Adelaja/Reuters
Andy Burnham says he wants ‘honest discussion’ about freedom of movement with EU
Prime minister is considering different options for new relationship with Brussels ahead of summit in November
Andy Burnham says he wants an “honest discussion” with the British public about the possibility of a return to free movement between the UK and the EU as part of his plans for a new relationship with the bloc.
The prime minister has been open about his desire to revisit the relationship between Britain and Brussels. He has said a new deal could mean the UK goes “all the way” and rejoins the EU.
Other options discussed by Burnham include joining the single market, which would require signing up to free movement, or being part of the customs union. Both would require an element of agreeing to rules set by the EU.
In an interview with the Sunday Times, he said: “I think you have to have a very factual, quite evidence-based debate about this.”
Burnham has previously committed to the red lines in Labour’s 2024 election manifesto, which ruled out the return of free movement, single market membership or a customs union.
It means any fundamental change in the relationship is likely to have to wait until after a general election, expected by 2029.
Burnham acknowledged freedom of movement had “very significant challenges associated with it”, but he suggested that European migration had changed in the past decade.
“It was a type of immigration that perhaps wasn’t as difficult [as now],” he told the Sunday Times . “Revolving-door migration where people would come and then go back is different. The balance of migration has changed, hasn’t it, since then? I think we’ve just got to have an honest discussion about those things.”
Burnham will meet EU leaders at a summit in Brussels on 20 November.
He argued there had been reciprocal benefits of free movement for Britons, particularly for young members of the population wanting to experience living in Europe.
Andy Burnham promises ‘serious dialogue’ over UK’s relationship with EU
Migration was one of the dominant topics of debate during the 2016 Brexit referendum campaign. In the 12 months to June 2016 – the month of the Brexit leave vote – net migration was at a near-record high of 335,000, according to the Office for National Statistics .
Decisions made after the Brexit result would lead to higher levels of migration, however. Net migration peaked at a record 944,000 in the year to March 2023 due to a surge of foreign workers encouraged to come to the UK by Boris Johnson’s Conservative government as the UK emerged from the Covid pandemic.
It has fallen sharply since then, with net migration dropping to well below pre-Brexit levels. It stood at 171,000 last year , almost half of what it was in 2016.
Burnham has said he will take the opportunity around the summit with EU leaders in November to lay out to the British public different options for the future relationship with the bloc.
He said he could not imagine recommending that the UK join the Schengen area, in which border controls have been abolished, or ditching the pound to adopt the euro.
Nigel Farage said the prime minister’s comments were a “total betrayal” of voters who backed Brexit , including in Burnham’s Makerfield constituency.
The Reform UK leader added: “He is also deluded to say immigration was different when we were members of the EU. We will fight this dishonesty.”
Chris Philp, the shadow home secretary, said: “Andy Burnham knows full well that a return to EU free movement would mean handing back control of our borders and allowing uncontrolled migration from across Europe. The British people voted to end free movement, and there can be no return to it.”
Youth mobility scheme may be delayed to ensure success of UK-EU summit
EU leaders to press Burnham to pay more towards Ukraine’s defence
